| 0.1.8k | 83 of 1385 real answers | client answered every other round | early reply restored |
| 0.1.8l | 861 of 1389 collected | host read 62% | clock skew again; keep/budget not the lever |
| 0.1.8m | 896 of 1511 | host evicted client at 2.0s | ~2s timeout, transport not the cause |
| 0.1.8n | 874 of 1554, acks lied 44% | host evicted client at 2.1s | reply keep fixed the lying acks |
| 0.1.8o | 1682 correct handoffs + 1674 bogus-length | host evicted client at 2.1s | 3354-byte mystery led to... |
| 0.1.8p | 9196 of 9202 on harness; length bug found | host evicted client at 2.1s | channel-stamp/length field order fixed |
| **0.1.8q** | **967 of 970, ~982 unique commands, no stalls** | **client's game left after 4.3s** | **game-level protocol (answer never changes)** |

Next: read the 46-byte command bodies the host's game sends (they are in the
log; the `[link-trace]` line does not carry them, so add a one-per-seq body dump
on the host's `tx cmd` path) and compare against what a real MKDS DS-to-DS
exchange carries; and log the guest's game's *reads* of the commands it is
delivered, which requires counting `W_RXBufDataRead` reads (zero today, meaning
the games use DMA or the RX buffer cursor - needs the right instrument).

---

### v0.1.8p - The host spent a frame and a half receiving every reply (2026-09-25)

Tenth attempt, and the first one where a *number* rather than a behaviour gives the
bug away. The v0.1.8o delivery log printed the frames the host's game is handed as
`3354 byte frame, fc=0118, as an MP host` - next to the `handing client 1's reply
... 26 byte frame, body[2]=00 00` line describing the very same call. A 3354-byte
reply made no sense, and the chase for it started from the frame's *destination*
and ended at the frame's *length*:

`StartRX` reads the frame length as a **16-bit** value at `RXBuffer[8]`, so byte 9
is the high half of it. The channel-transparency stamp from v0.1.8c is a single
byte written at 9 - and in `MPClientReplyRX` (the host's reply path) it was written
*after* the length:

```
*(u16*)&RXBuffer[8] = framelen;   // 26
RXBuffer[9] = (u8)CurChannel;     // ...and byte 9 of that 16-bit length
```

so the host's hardware read the length as `(channel << 8) | 26` - **3354** on
channel 13, 1818 on channel 7, different in every run, which is why it looked like
stale memory. `StartRX` then set `RXTime = length * 4` (the 2Mbps reply rate):
**13.4ms of emulated time to receive one 208us reply**, with `ComStatus` held busy
for the whole of it, so the host could not start the next frame's reception until
most of a frame had passed. The client was untouched - `CheckRX` writes its stamp
*before* the same length field, which is exactly why the client's deliveries in the
same log were sane 30- and 28-byte frames.

Fix: write the channel stamp before the length in `MPClientReplyRX`, so the length
field is what `StartRX` and the game read. Harness, same NSMB script from the same
save, 12000 frames, keep=2/notify=1: **answered 9202, collected 9196, empty
windows 6, canned 0, blank 0, stale 0, discards 0, 3.29x realtime** - against
1630 answered / 1587 collected / **801 empty** at 3000 frames and ~2016 collected /
~950 empty at 12000 frames before the fix. The host's rounds are no longer paced by
a phantom 13.4ms reception, so the same 12000 frames carry 4.5x as many MP rounds
and every one of them gets its answer. Smoke 66/0. Live MKDS still unproven at the
time of writing - and note this bug was *ours*: it arrived with the channel fix
(v0.1.8c) and is invisible to every counter that only looks at the wire.

---

### v0.1.8o - The answer the guest's game was never told it sent, and a 3354-byte frame on the host (2026-09-25)

Ninth attempt, and the first time the *guest's game* has been watched instead of
the wire. A new line reports every write to the reply-arm register with the bytes
it holds (`[mp] guest arms an answer: slot ... body[...]`), and a counter reports
every packet delivered to a console's own software
(`[mp] packet delivered to the console's own software: ... byte frame, fc=..., as
an MP client|host|outside multiplayer`). Two facts came out of the working NSMB
reference that reframe the whole MKDS problem:

1. **A working session arms exactly one answer.** Over 3000 frames the guest's
game wrote `W_TXSlotReply1` **once** (twice with stock's round-end reply path),
set its AID once, and answered all ~1700 rounds - every one of them a repeat of
the same command - from that single armed template. The host repeating one
byte-identical command for a whole session is not a stall: it is what NSMB's
working 2P link does too. So "the command is stuck" was never the symptom it
looked like, and an answer that does not change is not by itself a bug.
2. **Neither game reads its receive buffer.** Zero reads of `W_RXBufDataRead`
(both consoles, 3000 frames, and in the 66-check smoke test) while packets are
delivered to them, so what the emulated games do with MP traffic cannot be read
from the wire at all.

What the early reply (v0.1.8k) does differently is visible against stock: it
sends inside the round but skips the reply-slot transfer, and with it every
effect the hardware has on the *guest* when its answer leaves - the TX status
word (`RAM[replybuf] = 0x0001`, which stock writes one round late in
`SendMPReply`), the RF status, the `TXBusy` bit, and the completion interrupt
(`ProcessTX`, `num == 5`: `W_TXStat = 0x0401` + IRQ1 when `W_TXStatCnt` bit 12 is
set). `MD_EARLY_REPLY_ACK` (default 1) now performs both. Honest result: on the
NSMB harness it changes nothing measurable - answered 77202/77101, collected
73437/73330, zero blanks, zero canned replies, and the one-shot line reports
`W_TXStatCnt=0000`, i.e. the NSMB guest's game had not enabled that interrupt at
all. It is shipping as the *faithful* behaviour, not as a proven fix.

Two negative results, recorded so they are not retried:

- **Consuming the arm is not the handshake.** With `MD_EARLY_REPLY_ACK=2` the
guest still never re-armed: 1645 of 3000 rounds fell back to the firmware's
canned `fc=0158` reply, and the arm count stayed at 1.
- **Telling the guest's game about retries is not the lever either.**
`MD_CMD_RETRY_NOTIFY=1` delivers all 1737 repeats of the host's command (the new
`cmd from host:` line counts them) and the guest still arms once. It costs
nothing measurable (answered 1633 vs 1648, collected 1577 vs 1560 at 3000 frames)
and it is the only setting in which the guest's game is told the host is still
asking, so live runs now use it - but the frozen answer is not caused by it.

**The new lead is on the host, and it is malformed input, not lost input.** In
the same working NSMB session the host's game is handed each client reply
*twice*: once correctly (`[mp] handing client 1's reply to the console's game:
26 byte frame, fc=0118, body[2]=00 00`, 1682 times) and once as
`packet delivered to the console's own software: N so far, 3354 byte frame,
fc=0118, as an MP host` (1674 times). A 3354-byte frame control 0118 is not
something any console built - the reply is 30 bytes - so the host's receive queue
is being fed a second, impossible frame for every answer, and MKDS's host state
machine is the one that has to make sense of it. The client is handed only its
own commands (30 bytes, `fc=0228`) and acks (28 bytes, `fc=0218`), both sane.
Next: find which of `CheckRX`'s delivery paths produces that frame (the regular
path's `FinishRX` length is the frame length, so 3354 arrives from somewhere
else) and keep MP frames off it.

Smoke 66/0 throughout; the live app was rebuilt and restarted on this build with
`MD_CMD_RETRY_NOTIFY=1`, every delivery listed rather than sampled, so one more
join attempt names the source of that frame.

---


## Lessons from mgba-splitscreen

Detailed implementation guidance based on the mgba-splitscreen project:

### 1. Architecture

**mgba-splitscreen approach:**
```
libmgba (C) — mGBA core, compiled as static library
Rust backend (src-tauri) — wraps libmgba via bindgen, manages instances, runs frame loop, serves frames + input over WebSocket
Frontend (src) — HTML/JS canvas UI, renders screens, maps input to GBA buttons
```

**melonDS Multiplayer should follow:**
```
melonDS core (C++) — NDS emulation, Wi-Fi link cable
Rust backend (src-tauri) — wraps melonDS, manages 2-4 instances, frame loop, WebSocket
Frontend (src) — HTML/JS canvas UI, renders screens side by side, handles input
```

**Why this works:**
- Clean separation: emulation in C++, app logic in Rust, UI in web tech
- WebSocket allows same frontend to work in browser and Tauri app
- Easy to test frontend independently

### 2. View Modes (Essential Feature)

mgba-splitscreen implemented four view modes, all essential:

| Mode | Description | Use Case |
|------|-------------|----------|
| **Grid** | All screens equal size, side by side | 2-4 players, fair view |
| **Speaker** | 1 large screen + smaller others | Competitive games, one player needs larger view |
| **Focus** | Single screen, full size | Watching, solo play, presentation |
| **Overlay/PiP** | Picture-in-picture, compact | Small screens, monitoring multiple |

**Implementation notes:**
- Cycle through modes with hotkey (F8/F9 in mgba-splitscreen)
- Should be remappable
- Each mode needs different canvas layout calculations
- Background image option is nice-to-have

### 3. Controls Design

**mgba-splitscreen control scheme:**

Player 1 (left hand): W/A/S/D for D-Pad, K for A, J for B, H for L, L for R, Enter for Start, Backspace for Select

Player 2 (right hand/arrows): Arrow keys for D-Pad, M for A, N for B, V for L, B for R, P for Start, O for Select

Players 3-4: Additional clusters (T/G/F/R and I/Q/C/E zones)

**Key lessons:**
- Keyboard zones should be intuitive (left hand vs right hand)
- All controls must be fully remappable
- Global hotkeys (turbo, save/load, pause) should also be remappable
- Gamepad support: auto-assign controllers to player slots

**For melonDS:**
- Player 1: WASD + nearby keys (left side of keyboard)
- Player 2: Arrow keys + nearby keys (right side of keyboard)
- Players 3-4: Additional zones or rely on gamepads
- Consider DS's dual-screen nature: touch screen input for bottom screen

### 4. Save State Management

**mgba-splitscreen approach:**
- Quick save (F5): Captures ALL players' states together
- Quick load (F7): Restores ALL players' states together
- Battery saves: Auto-loaded from .sav files next to ROM
- Save import/export: Per instance or as a set across all running instances

**Why global save/load matters:**
- In multiplayer games, all players need to be at the same point
- Separate save states would cause desync
- Loading one player's state without others breaks the game

**For melonDS:**
- Quick save: Save all NDS instance states to one file
- Quick load: Load all states from one file
- Battery saves: .sav files per instance, auto-loaded

### 5. Game Library UX

**mgba-splitscreen features:**
- Recent games list (persisted)
- ROM folder scanning (add folders to library)
- Box art display (local sibling images or online fallback)
- Controller/keyboard navigation in library
- Launch with player count selection from library

**Implementation guidance:**
- Scan ROM folders for .nds files
- Extract title from ROM header for display
- Look for box art images (PNG/JPG) with same name as ROM
- Fallback to online box art service if no local image
- Store game library config persistently (localStorage or file)

### 6. Pause Menu

**mgba-splitscreen pause menu:**
- Pausing (Escape) freezes ALL players at once
- Menu is controller-navigable
- Options: Resume, Save, Load, Players, Library, Quit ROM

**Why pause all players:**
- In multiplayer, one player pausing should pause everyone
- Prevents one player from gaining advantage by pausing

### 7. Turbo Mode

**mgba-splitscreen:**
- Turbo (Q key): Fast-forward past 60fps for grinding through menus/animations
- Useful for: navigating game menus, fast-forwarding through dialogue, grinding

**For melonDS:**
- Implement similar turbo mode
- Should affect all instances equally (or be per-player?)

### 8. Deep Links / URL Parameters

**mgba-splitscreen:**
- `?players=N` — Start with N players (1-4, default 2)
- `?rom=<url>` — Fetch and start a ROM immediately

**For melonDS:**
- `?players=N` — Useful for quick launch
- Could add `?rom=` for direct launch

### 9. Platform Support

**mgba-splitscreen platforms:**
- **Desktop (Tauri)**: Native app, full performance
- **Web (WASM)**: Full emulator in browser, no install
- **Android (Tauri)**: Thin shell around WASM engine

**For melonDS:**
- **Desktop (Tauri)** — Primary target
- **Web (WASM)** — Optional, could be future enhancement
- **Android** — Optional, tablets/TVs could benefit

### 10. Build Considerations

**mgba-splitscreen build notes:**
- First build compiles entire libmgba from source (takes minutes, several GB RAM)
- Subsequent builds are incremental
- Android build is fast because it uses WASM, not native compilation

**For melonDS:**
- melonDS is already large; consider build times
- Static linking of melonDS core into Tauri app
- CMake for melonDS, cargo for Rust/Tauri

---

## Relationship to Original melonDS

melonDS Multiplayer is a derivative work based on melonDS. Key points:

1. **License**: Inherits GNU GPL v3 from melonDS
2. **Code Base**: Uses melonDS emulator core
3. **Architecture**: Adds Tauri wrapper for splitscreen display
4. **Independence**: Separate project with different goals (splitscreen vs single-player)

### Upstream Relationship
- `upstream` remote: https://github.com/melonDS-emu/melonDS.git
- Track upstream for emulator core updates
- Adapt changes as needed for splitscreen architecture

### Credits
Based on melonDS by Arisotura (https://github.com/melonDS-emu/melonDS)

Additional credits from original melonDS:
- Martin for GBAtek documentation
- Cydrak for 3D GPU research
- limittox for the icon
- Community testers and contributors

**Inspired by:** [mgba-splitscreen](https://github.com/Spuds0588/mgba-splitscreen) — demonstrated the viability of emulator-based splitscreen multiplayer.

---

## Future Milestones

### Near-term Goals (Phase 1-2)
1. Implement the local link between instances — the core of the product
2. Wire the Rust side of the Tauri app to the C bridge
3. Pipe framebuffers to the webview and draw one screen per player
4. Extend to multiple screens side by side
5. Implement view modes (grid, speaker, focus, overlay)
6. Decide and enforce the emulator threading model

### Medium-term Goals (Phase 3-4)
1. Per-player keyboard controls with remapping
2. Gamepad support
3. Game library with box art
4. Save state management (global save/load)
5. Pause menu
6. Testing with multiplayer games

### Long-term Goals (Phase 5+)
1. Beta release
2. Optional: WebRTC for remote players
3. Optional: Android/Web support
4. Additional view mode customization

## Document History

| Date | Version | Author | Changes |
|------|---------|--------|---------|
| 2026-10-20 | 0.1.9ds | **Pinned to one, two and four cores, the hardware rule gets a number it never had: MKDS holds full speed for one console at two cores and for two consoles at eight, and never for four - not because the cores run out (four consoles use 3.9 of 8) but because one console's frame in the 3D race costs 19-20 ms of its own thread, above the 16.7 ms a 60 fps frame allows.** The light 60 fps `diag.nds` ROM is the other half of the answer: four of those hold at two cores. At one core nothing MKDS holds full speed. Asked to find where the limit actually is by pinning the release binary to a 1-, 2- and 4-thread budget with the recorded diag and MKDS recipes, then to watch what the product does when it cannot keep up and fix the single cheapest real defect. **The budget is a real one for the whole tree.** `systemd-run --user --scope -p CPUQuota=N00%` wraps the launch, so the cap covers the app *and* the WebKit web process - the page is a separate process, and pinning only the app with `taskset` leaves the entire display path outside the budget (the same run reads 48.6 emulated at a one-core quota and 57.2 pinned with `taskset`, so the quota is the stricter model and is what the table uses). CPU is the summed `/proc/<pid>/stat` of the tree (three processes: the app, WebKitWebProcess, WebKitNetworkProcess). Same recipe otherwise: MKDS `0168` title/attract and `diag.nds`, autostart N consoles, emulated fps from the five-second health windows, page fps from the session's `.fps` spans over the control channel, 20-30 s per point. **The table: MKDS, five-second-window range over the run / tree cores / painted fps.** The attract loop has two phases and they behave completely differently - the title and menu windows run at 60 even on one core, the 3D race is the load - so each cell gives the window range and, in brackets, how many windows were at full speed. 1 core: **1 console 52.0-60.0 (3/8) / 0.92 / 54 · 2 consoles 35.2-60.0 (3/7) / 0.98 / 28 + 18 · 4 consoles 20.8-43.0 (0/7) / 1.00 / 28 + 18**. 2 cores: **1 console 59.6-60.0 (8/8) / 0.96 / 59 · 2 consoles 44.8-60.0 (4/8) / 1.66 / 57 + 30 · 4 consoles 29.4-60.0 (3/7) / 1.94 / 26 + 18**. 4 cores: **1 console 59.6-60.0 (8/8) / 0.95 / 59 · 2 consoles 56.2-60.0 (4/8) / 1.85 / 57 + 31 · 4 consoles 35.2-60.0 (4/8) / 3.49 / 38 + 30**. 8 cores: **1 console 59.6-60.0 (6/6) / 0.87 / 60 · 2 consoles 59.0-60.0 (5/7) / 1.73 / 59 + 31 · 4 consoles 39.8-60.0 (4/8) / 3.87 / 42 + 31**. `diag.nds`, same budgets: 1 core **1 console 59.8-60.0 / 0.52 / 60 · 2 consoles 59.4-60.0 / 0.76 / 60 + 30 · 3 consoles 56.4-59.8 / 0.98 / 57 + 30 · 4 consoles 48.2-49.6 / 1.00 / 39 + 24**; 2 and 4 cores **4 consoles 59.8-60.2 / 1.31 / 60 + 30** at both. **Where the limit actually is, measured.** In the race scene at four consoles on the whole box every console ran **45.7 fps with its own thread at 0.85-0.90 cores - 19.0-19.6 ms of CPU per frame** - while the main thread took 0.24 and the link's ReceiveQueue 0.23 (3.97 cores of 8 in total, no other thread near saturation). A frame is due every 16.7 ms, so **a single console's own run-loop thread is already over the frame budget in that scene**: more cores cannot lift it, and the four-console number moves only 35 → 40 from 4 to 8 cores because the extra cores clean up the page's and the driver's share, not the consoles'. Two consoles on the whole box run 59.0-60.0 at 16.9 ms per frame, right at the edge; one console runs 59.6 at 2 cores. So the answer per budget - the largest count that holds ~59.9 through the worst scene - is **0 consoles at 1 core, 1 at 2 cores, 1 at 4 (two dip to 56 in the race), 2 at 8** for MKDS, and **2 at 1 core, 4 at 2 and 4 cores** for `diag.nds`. The lever the hardware rule needs is therefore **per-console frame cost** (a smaller game, less rendering per frame), not a larger core budget: the same box that cannot hold four MKDS consoles holds four diag ones on two cores. **What the product does when it is over budget** (live at a one-core quota, two consoles, MKDS 3D): consoles stay in **exact lockstep** (122 and 123 frames over 3 s, health windows identical for both), the watchdog **stays quiet** - no kick, no resync, no deauth, no false "wedged" - input still lands (a 700 ms `press a` took 0.76 s wall and moved that console's own keypad register), the control channel still reads the screen in 0.12 s, and **the picture is the casualty**: the game ran 41 fps while the cards showed 15-28, and the status line went on reading "running · link up 30s · slots 0b00". Nothing else in the window says the machine is short. Audio is not on the local path at all (the SPU is drained by `get_audio`, which the page polls only while hosting online), so no audio stall is observable and local splitscreen has no sound - recorded as a gap, not fixed here. **The one fix: say it.** `frontend/index.html` now computes each console's emulated rate from the frame counter `link_status` already reports, next to the painted rate it already computes, and names the shortfall in the status line: `over budget: game 41 fps, picture 15 fps · this machine cannot hold 2 consoles at full speed`. It needs three consecutive seconds to appear and three good ones to clear (a scene that merely dips must not flash), it replaces the link report only while it holds, and it is deliberately quiet between 55 and 59.9 fps so an attract-scene wobble on a capable machine does not cry wolf; the picture-behind arm fires when the picture is below 65% of the game's own rate. **Proved live, in both directions:** at a one-core quota with two consoles the line read 11/11 samples with `game 27-32 fps, picture 25-29 fps` against health windows of 33-37 (the poll itself costs a little, so the page's figure is the lower of the two); on the whole box it appeared 0/8 for one console (game 60), 0/5 for four diag consoles (60) and, at four MKDS consoles, after its three seconds and then steadily (`game` ~50, `picture` ~42). `node --check` clean on the page's inline script; `cargo test --lib` **54 passed, 0 failed**; `cargo build --release` clean. **The sessions' own health lines** are in [logs/cpu_budget_20261020.log.gz](logs/cpu_budget_20261020.log.gz) (21 sessions, 377 windows). **Limits.** 20-30 s per point, so each point is a scene sample rather than a ten-minute average - which is why the cells carry window ranges and a full-window count instead of one median that straddles the two phases; the quota model limits CPU time, not placement, so it is stricter than a pin and not identical to a real low-power part; the per-frame cost is measured for MKDS's race scene only, and whether the same 19-20 ms ceiling is what a four-player *joined* room hits is the next question. Changed: `frontend/index.html` (the over-budget line), `history.md`, `to-do.md`, `logs/cpu_budget_20261020.log.gz` (new). Committed and pushed to `master`. |
| 2026-10-20 | 0.1.9dt | **The 19-20 ms a console thread burned per 3D frame is now split by section, and 44% of it was the software rasteriser painting pixels in sequence with the CPU that owns it sitting idle - fixable without touching the rasteriser, because melonDS's own frontend already runs the soft 3D renderer on a thread (`3D.Soft.Threaded` is `true` in its `Config.cpp`) and this bridge never told the renderer anything. Wiring `SetRenderSettings` takes the race scene from 33-35 to 46-53 fps at four consoles, with the picture identical frame for frame.** Asked to stop inferring the 19-20 ms ceiling and profile it, then land one measured reduction. **Method (the timers were rented for this pass and removed).** `FrameProf.h` included by the bridge and the core, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` pairs around each section, gated on `MD_FRAME_PROF` (a `getenv` once per thread; the first attempt used `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID` at ~2000 syscalls per frame and slowed the session from 45 fps to 9 - the profiler measuring itself), printing a `[prof]` line every 100 frames, plus matching `Instant` pairs in the Rust frame loop for what sits outside `md_run_frame` (`[host]`: barrier, pacing sleep, picture copy). Four MKDS consoles, autostart, whole box, race scene, with the five-second health windows running in parallel. **The split, ms per console thread per frame** (`period` 27.2 ms = one frame): `rframe`, the 3D rasteriser, **12.4** - setup 0.18, the 192-line render 11.9, the fill itself (every `RenderPolygonScanline` call) **10.7**, the final pass (edge marking, fog) 0.14 - so the per-polygon dispatch loop around the fill is 1.25; 2D `drawline` 2.2 + `sprites` 0.45; ARM9 2.15, ARM7 0.88; `gpu3d` display-list geometry 2.3; SPU 0.48; scheduler and everything else 2.7; link/wifi/cart 0.03. Total **24.4 of 27.2**, and the rest is the lockstep **barrier at 2.7-3.8 ms**, a pacing sleep of **0** (this thread is behind, not early) and a 0.10 ms picture copy. **So the largest item is genuine per-pixel work and the only slack in it is that 1.25 ms dispatch loop** - shading a 256x192 scene with texturing, fog and edge marking is what the 10.7 ms is. **The one change: give the rasteriser its own thread.** `src/frontend/tauri/Bridge.cpp` now calls `GetRenderer().SetRenderSettings(...)` with `Threaded = true` after `Reset()`, in both `md_load_rom` and `md_reset` - the setting upstream's Qt frontend applies by default - so a console's 3D frame is produced while its CPU emulates the lines before it and the 2D compositor waits on the existing per-line semaphore (`GetLine`, `Sema_ScanlineCount`, `FinishRendering` at VCOUNT 192); same rasteriser, same order, same buffers. **Four consoles, MKDS race phase, same recipe:** serial **33-35** fps (windows 40, 33, 34, 35) -> threaded **41-48** in the A/B (48, 41, 43, 43) and **46-53** (53, 46, 50, 50, 48) on the shipped binary in a 35 s run; the four consoles' windows are identical to each other in every run, so lockstep is untouched. `lcd` (the HBlank callback that holds the 3D render) drops 7.0 -> 3.4 ms and the console thread's own total 13.8 -> 11.7 ms averaged over both phases; the tree costs **4.06 -> 5.02 cores** - it buys frames with one extra thread per console, which is the hardware rule's own trade (frames for threads, not accuracy). **The picture is identical - checked, not assumed.** CRC32 of both screens every 60 frames, one MKDS console: serial twice and threaded once, **14/14 identical** at the same frame numbers (including the moving attract scenes at frames 120-300); four consoles threaded, **p1..p4 identical at every sampled frame**, and equal to the single-console serial hashes at those frame numbers; the pinned ROM's `.sav` sha256 `6efe13d9ad59...` unchanged before and after the runs; `md_smoke_test` **176 checks, 0 failures** and `md_capture` **12 captures, 0 failures** (both now drive the threaded path), `cargo test --lib` **54 passed, 0 failed**, release build clean from scratch of the bridge. **Limits.** The scene samples are MKDS attract/race windows of 16-35 s, not a joined four-player race - the join was not re-driven this pass; four consoles in the worst scene are still 46-53 fps, not 60, and the 10.7 ms fill is untouched (the change hides it behind the CPU rather than shrinking it), so the next item on that thread is the 2.7 ms 2D compositing plus the 2.3 ms geometry stage; the `[host]` split was measured on one box only. Evidence: `logs/cpu_profile_20261020.log.gz` (8 session logs, local - `logs/` is not tracked). Changed: `src/frontend/tauri/Bridge.cpp`, `history.md`, `to-do.md`. Committed and pushed to `master`. |
| 2026-10-20 | 0.1.9dr | **The app frame rates this record has been comparing were taken with the machine in its power-saving profile. Re-measured with the profile set to performance and the same recipe, four consoles of MKDS read 48.4 / 50.8 / 46.9 emulated fps in three separate samples against 14.4, at 3.24-3.45 cores instead of 3.99, and the four-console `diag.nds` pull costs 6.0 ms instead of 27.6.** Asked to re-measure through the real surface under the performance profile - emulated fps per console, host CPU and page fps for four consoles, plus one- and two-console points so the curve is directly comparable, and the four-console diag-ROM pull cost - then to correct every throttled figure in `history.md`/`to-do.md` while keeping the old number visible next to the new one. **The machine's state, recorded with the numbers because it changed and nobody had written it down:** Intel i5-8350U (4 cores / 8 threads, 1.7 GHz base), all 8 threads with `energy_performance_preference` at `performance` and `scaling_governor` at `powersave` - intel_pstate's only governor name, so not evidence of throttling on its own - holding **3600 MHz** in 23 of 24 one-second samples taken during a four-console run. The recipe is unchanged from v0.1.9do/dp: MKDS `0168 - Mario Kart DS (U)(SCZ).nds` at the title/attract scene, N consoles autostarted, emulated fps from the app's own five-second health windows (`frames X(+Y)` -> Y/5), page fps from the session's `.fps` spans read over the control channel, host CPU from `/proc/<pid>/stat` over the sampling window, 40-45 s per point (181 s for the long one). **The scaling curve.** 1 console **59.9 fps / 0.70 cores / page 59**; 2 consoles **59.3, 59.3 / 1.54 / page 58 focused + 30 unfocused**; 4 consoles **48.4 / 3.45 / 45 + 31**, a second 46 s sample **50.8 / 3.38 / 47 + 31**, and a third 181 s sample **46.9 / 3.24 / 48 + 31** with **39 windows, min 39.8 / median 46.9 / max 60**, equal on all four consoles window for window; side mode at four **41.5 / 3.60 / page 40 + 30**. `diag.nds` on the same profile: one all-seat pull **2.0 / 3.0 / 6.0 ms** at 1 / 2 / 4 consoles for **196,628 / 393,252 / 786,500 bytes** while the consoles ran 60.2 fps (301 frames in the first five-second window). **The figures these replace, kept beside them with their caveat rather than overwritten:** 1 console 31.9 fps / 1.22 cores / page 31; 2 consoles 28.6 / 2.20 / 22; 4 consoles 14.4 / 3.99 / 15; a 118-window distribution of min 13.4 / median 19.9 / max 55.8; all-seat pull 7.6 / 14.8 / 27.6 ms; side mode 28 fps / 3.81 cores. **What that corrects in the record, and what it does not.** The present-path reading survives with more room: the display path is still not the limit, because four consoles paint 45-48 while the emulator runs 46.9-50.8, so the RGB555 and request-what-is-shown change is still what made the pump cost what is on screen. What does not survive is "the emulator is the limit" as a ceiling: two consoles now run 59.3 of 60 and four run 46.9-50.8, and the "webview's own ~31 Hz refresh cap" recorded in v0.1.9do was the throttled compositor rather than the webview - one console paints at 59 here, while the unfocused consoles' 30 Hz is the page's own deliberate cadence. *The caveat that now attaches to every older figure:* the earlier rows do not record the power profile and the user reports it was power saving, so v0.1.9do's 29-31 painted / 3.21 cores and 7.6/14.8/27.6 ms, v0.1.9dp's MKDS numbers and its 118-window distribution, the pre-rebuild 43.1/33.2/26.6, and the hardware rule's "~2.2x realtime" are **lower bounds taken on a slower clock, not a different build** - none of those runs measured the clock, and the new table above is the same code with the profile on. **The module split is committed with this pass.** Frame and audio payloads and the two commands that answer with them live in `src-tauri/src/frames.rs` (197 lines), the stall-kick policy `kick_after_windows`/`kick_allowed` and its two tests in `src-tauri/src/link_watch.rs` (96), and the payload decoder the page and the control channel share in `frontend/frame-codec.js` (76); `lib.rs` 5805 -> 5540 lines, `index.html` 2040 -> 1979, `browser_host.rs` gained the `/frame-codec.js` route and the `crate::frames::` paths, and two doc comments stranded by earlier edits were put back on the functions they describe. Behaviour is identical where it can be measured: the same 2-console request answers **294,948 bytes, 2 entries, `[[0,1],[1,3]]`** before and after, the moved codec decodes it, and against a live 2-console MKDS session the moved decoder backs `see`, `digest` and `shot` (512x384 shot, both columns 333 colours, 98% non-black). **The runs' own health lines** are in [logs/perf_profile_20261020.log.gz](logs/perf_profile_20261020.log.gz) (nine sessions: 1, 2, 4, 4-side and the 181 s four-console run, plus the diag pulls at 1/2/4), 343 windows in all. **Checks.** `cargo test --lib` **54 passed, 0 failed** on the final tree; `cargo build --release` clean; `node --check` on `frontend/frame-codec.js`, `frontend/online.js` and the page's inline script; the published `guest.html` and `online.js` sha256-match the working tree byte for byte and the README's web-version link answers 200 (the Pages workflow publishes only the three guest files, and this pass does not touch them). **Limits.** The four-console numbers are the attract scene with four consoles started together, not v0.1.9dp's *joined* room, whose ten-minute distribution has not been re-run on this profile; the side-mode point is one 40 s sample; and the clock claim is the sustained frequency during one of the runs, not a package-power measurement. Committed: `02027a13` (the module split), with this measurement record as its companion commit, on `master`. |
| 2026-10-20 | 0.1.9dq | **The online join is settled: a foreground guest joins the browser host by code and by invite link from the published guest page, decodes the streamed console, and its button press moves that console's own keypad register (03FF → 03FE → 03FF) - and the ambiguity the last pass recorded is now measured rather than guessed.** Asked to settle a guest that sat on "connecting…" for ~40 s while the host saw no join request, by running both ends in foreground windows with the emulator running and reading the host's own seat state, then to fix the handshake where it lives so the failure is impossible rather than less likely. **The join works.** Real Chrome (headless, driven over CDP; `frontend/index.html` served by the app's browser host on loopback) hosting, and the published `https://spuds0588.github.io/melonDS-Multiplayer/guest.html` as the guest: by code the guest asked (`role=guest`), the host's own state showed `pending: 1` and the panel "P2 wants to join — accept", the accept moved the host to `seats: [{seat: 1}]` / "hosting · 1 of 1 seats taken" / chip "P2 online", and the guest reported `welcome`, `seat: 1`, 10 frames / 98 KB, 48,977 of 98,304 canvas pixels lit (so it decoded, not merely received); by invite link the published page was admitted on its own token (`hello`) with no approval and streamed 12 frames. Input in both cases: a guest's A press moved the emulated console's own keypad register from `03FF` to `03FE` and back to `03FF` on release (`appctl probe --player 2`, a `keyinput` field added this pass), with p1's register untouched. **What was actually broken - three defects, each reproduced before it was fixed, none of them the handshake itself.** (1) *PeerJS drops its own broker socket and nulls the peer id.* When the signaller goes away (an idle host, a laptop that slept, a network change) PeerJS calls `disconnect()`, sets `peer.id = null` and stays that way: existing guests keep streaming (the datachannel is peer to peer) while the join code and every invite minted from then on point at a host that is no longer registered - a host that looks live and cannot be reached, which is exactly the shape of last pass's report. Reproduced on the browser host (`stats().peerId` reading null while a seat was mid-stream). (2) *A vanished guest kept its seat forever.* Measured: 30+ s after the guest's tab closed the host still reported `guests: 1`, its send counter still climbing; the connection's `close` event never arrived. (3) *A guest that could not complete had no reason.* If the datachannel never opened, the page stayed on "connecting…" with nothing else, and a failed join left the code box hidden so retrying needed a reload. Two smaller ones came out of the same runs: an invite died with the guest that used it (`releaseSeat` deleted the seat's invite and never re-minted, so a host could invite exactly one player per console per session), and `stats().seats` kept listing a departed seat. **Fixes, all in the transport and the panel.** A guest join is now bounded: 20 s without an open datachannel, or a peer/connection error, aborts with a reason the player can act on ("no host is answering that code (unavailable)"), resets to idle so a new code can be typed, and the overlay says "Not connected" with the reason instead of a spinner. The host pings each connection every 2.5 s and drops a seat it has not heard from for 8 s - measured **6.1-6.6 s** from the guest tab closing to `guests: 0`, chip "P2 free", invite re-minted - and only a guest that has answered a ping can be dropped, so the published page (which predates the ping) is never dropped by it. `watchSignalling` reconnects with backoff: measured `signalling: down`, `peerId: null`, panel "hosting (signalling down)", code hidden and links reading "the host lost the signalling server - reconnecting, new guests cannot join until it is back", then **1.6 s** later the same peer id, the code back and a new guest joining by that code. Repaints stopped falling back to "0 of 1 seats taken" beside a live chip, and `releaseSeat` mints a fresh invite. **Checks.** `cargo test --lib` 54 passed, 0 failed; `node --check` on `online.js` and the page's inline script; the driver's full output is [logs/online_join_20261020.log](logs/online_join_20261020.log) (code join → keypad → seat drop → signalling drop/recover → join after recovery → invite from the published page). **Limits.** The published page still serves the previous `online.js` until this change is pushed (the Pages workflow publishes `frontend/guest.html`, `frontend/online.js` and the vendored PeerJS), so the new reasons and the ping/pong only reach real guests on that push; the measured join above used the published page as it is today and the fixed page from the app's own loopback. A host page that disappears (reload, closed tab) still leaves the last remote player's buttons held on that console, because the page owns the masks and nothing releases them on unload. Invites are single-use per seat by design, now re-minted per join. Changed: `frontend/online.js` (guest abort/timeout, ping/pong liveness, signalling recovery, `stats().signalling`), `frontend/guest.html` (a failed join shows its reason and keeps the code box), `frontend/index.html` (seat count on repaint, invite re-mint, code/links withheld while signalling is down), `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` (`keyinput` in `probe`), `tools/appctl.py` (prints it), `history.md`, `to-do.md`, `logs/online_join_20261020.log` (new). Nothing committed. |

| 2026-10-20 | 0.1.9do | **The frame pump now costs what is on screen: four consoles paint at the display's own cap (29-31 fps), host CPU falls from 3.57 to 3.21 cores and every console still runs ~59 fps - and a rejected pull costs one frame instead of the rest of the session.** Asked to rebuild the display path so its cost follows what is being watched, and to make a failed pull recoverable. **What indicted the old shape** (`diag.nds`, this machine, 8 cores): one `get_frames` pull took **13.0 ms at one console, 34.3 at two, 70.1 at four** (1,572,896 bytes, ~22 MB/s over IPC) while the consoles ran 59.9 fps; the page painted 31/31/24/**16** fps and the host held 1.06/2.05/2.86/**3.57** cores. Every console's two screens crossed as RGBA on every pull, the hidden screen included, and a rejected pull set `running = false` - every player's picture froze for the rest of the session while the emulation kept running. **What changed, at the request rather than at the canvas.** `get_frames` takes the seats and screens the page is about to paint (`FrameSeat`: bit 0 top, bit 1 bottom) and sends each pixel as **RGB555**, the DS's own 15-bit output - the page's expansion back to RGBA is lossless, the alpha byte was constant, and it is half the bytes. An empty request still means every seat and both screens, so the browser host's transport and the control channel's readers are unaffected. The page asks for the focused console every refresh and the others at 30 Hz, asks for nothing while the window is hidden, and repaints immediately when a peek or a layout switch reveals a screen; the idle deadline carries 6 ms of slack because a 33 ms slot on a 31 Hz display ran the unfocused consoles at 16 Hz (measured on the first arm). **One decoder.** The wire format is owned by `window.__mdframes.decode` in `frontend/index.html`; the control channel's `see`/`bands`/`shot`/`digest` rebuild their dense per-player buffers from it (`CTRL_PRELUDE`'s `D.unpack`), so there is no second copy of the format to drift and their readings keep their old shape. **A failed pull is now a dropped frame.** The catch no longer stops the pump: it says `frame pull failed (<why>) - retrying` and clears to `frame pull recovered after N failed pull(s)` when the next pull lands. `MD_FRAME_PULL_FAIL=N` (diagnostic, off by default, forwarded by `tools/run_app.sh`) rejects every Nth pull so the path can be exercised instead of assumed. **Measured after, same recipe** (page fps from the session's own `.fps` readouts, CPU from `/proc/<pid>/stat`, pull timed in the page): 1 console 31 fps / 0.75 cores / 7.6 ms per full pull; 2 consoles 31,31 / 1.47 / 14.8 ms; 3 consoles 31,30,30 / 2.19 / 24.4 ms; **4 consoles 29,29,29,29 fps at 3.21 cores** and 27.6 ms per full pull (786,500 bytes - the same all-seat request that took 70.1 ms before, 2.5x cheaper), emulated 58.7-59.1 fps on every console. Side mode (both screens, 4 consoles) paints 28 fps at 3.81 cores. **Exercised, not asserted:** the decoder was unit-checked through the page with hand-built payloads - red `0x001F`, green `0x03E0`, blue `0x7C00`, white, black all decode to exactly `[255,0,0,255]`... `[0,0,0,255]`, which pins channel order and the LUT independently of the emulator; with `MD_FRAME_PULL_FAIL=3` at two consoles the status line alternated `frame pull failed ... retrying` / `frame pull recovered after 1 failed pull(s)` while the page held 20 fps (two thirds of 31 - exactly the dropped frames) and the consoles kept running; `shot` at four consoles is 1024x384 with all four columns carrying distinct content (4/5/6/7 colours, 100% non-black) and four distinct `digest` values; `see` reads game text on consoles 1-4; `cargo test --lib` **52 passed, 0 failed**. Where it still costs more than it should: side mode at four consoles (28 fps, 3.81 cores) pays for eight `putImageData` calls a frame, and the webview's own refresh is capped at ~31 Hz in this session, so no arm can exceed that here. The previous pass disassembled at that raw value and named code 4 bytes off. **Arming is race-free by construction now:** `SetPcTrace` only publishes a request (release-incrementing `PcTraceReqGen`); `ApplyPcTraceRequest` runs on the CPU thread at the top of `RunFrame`; the recorder is fixed-size arrays only (`PcTraceRing[16384]` of `{pc,lr,cpsr,r0..r3}`), so nothing is reallocated or cleared from the control thread - the vector reallocation that caused the earlier SIGSEGV is gone, and each entry carries the registers at the visit. **Measured in the run that ships (JIT on, `logs/v9jit2.log.gz`, route exit 0, `BOOTED`, transfer byte-identical at 2502 packets / 1260340 of 1260340 bytes, 0 warns/errors):** child `0x027FFC40 = 0x00000002` (host `= 1`), so the test at `0x0201CA70` would divert (`ldrh r0,[0x027FFC40]; cmp r0,#2; bne skip; bl 0x0201CD34`) - but tight windows held 366 frames each record **0 visits** to `[0x0201CD34,0x0201CD48)` (the loop body) and `[0x0201CA70,0x0201CA8C)` (the test). A JIT block with a backward branch returns to the dispatcher each timeslice, and the child's own `0x0201C780` pacing loop is the positive control (~11k entries/frame), so 0 means never entered. The wide window shows the child's real steady cycle (`0x0201C780`->`0x0201C78C`->`0x0201C7F8`, `r3 += 0x40` per pass; the `0x0201C9D8`/`0x0201C9EC` IRQ helpers; then `0x0201CD30`), and the last entry `0x0201CD30` with `lr 0201AC10` is exactly the block entry the old raw `pc9 0201CD34` names - disassembly of the child's own dump (`logs/v9jit2_dump_cd.txt`) shows `0x0201CD30` is `bx lr`, the return from the cache-drain helper `0x0201CD28`. So the child is in an active main loop with IRQs serviced (`IE=2009 IME=0001 IF=0000 can_take=True`), not parked in the DP error path; its ARM7 stays in BIOS idle and `mp cmds tx 0`. **Honest result:** not "localized, not named" - the named site is disproved. The one remaining measurement: arm the trace before the hand-off so it spans the boot transition, not just the post-boot steady state. **Validation:** `cmake --build build-tauri -j8` exit 0; `cargo build --release` exit 0; `md_smoke_test tools/mkdiagrom/diag.nds` **159 checks, 0 failures**; `cargo test --lib` **39 passed, 0 failed**. `logs/v9jit2_finding.txt` digests the pass. Nothing committed. |

The trace shows the child ran state 16 - its `W_PowerForce <- 0` write carries `lr7 037F94C4`, the return address of that branch's own `bl` - and then state 0 (its last two writes are the park), where the working console goes on to `ModeWEP <- 000A`, `W_PowerUS <- 0`, `W_BBPower <- 0`, a second RF block, `ModeWEP <- 004A` and `W_PowerForce <- 8001/8000` and raises `TRX power ON` eight times. The child never runs `0x027EA66C` at all: its `W_PowerForce` offset has exactly one writer, `0x027EA4E8`. **The gate, as far as the trace can see, and why it is not an emulated value.** After the park the child's ARM7 returns to the BIOS SWI idle (`pc7 0000115C`, `lr7 037F84FC`) and never touches a wifi register again; its post-boot history is frozen at `W_BBBusy` 15460 reads, `W_BBRead` 1018, `W_RFBusy` 637 and **zero** reads of `W_IF`/`W_IE`/`W_USCount*`/`W_TXStat`/`W_CMDStat*`, with `TRX power ON` 0 against the host's 8 on the same run. | 2026-10-02 | 0.1.9da | **The downloaded title now starts the way a real boot starts - a whole-console `NDS::Reset()` before the image is written - and the client leaves the BIOS for good: its ARM9 runs inside the downloaded image on 96-99 of 100 samples with zero faults, its ARM7 runs the game's own ARM7 code, and the joiner no longer steals back the radio it powers. The requested outcome is still not met: the child's screen never advances.** Asked to find why control does not stay with the downloaded title after `SetupDownloadPlayBoot`, to compare the manual reset-and-jump against the emulator's own ROM start path, and to make the image start the way a real boot does. **The comparison was the answer, and the old path really did skip it.** `md_load_rom` runs `Reset()` -> `SetupDirectBoot(rom)` -> `Start()`; `SetupDownloadPlayBoot` ran neither end. **What named it was nondeterminism.** On the old path the child faulted a few hundred bytes into its entry *at a different address every run* - `undefined ARM9 instruction 9E340216 @ 02008BEC` (run 3), `ARM9: data abort (02000A28)` (run 4), `undefined ARM9 instruction 2D401002 @ 02008B98` (run 5) - which is the signature of reading stale RAM, not of a bad image: the image is provably self-consistent (the child's own header in RAM states ARM9 `0x10B324` + ARM7 `0x286B0` + header `0x160`, exactly the `1260340` bytes transferred) and its ARM7 at `02380000` was valid ARM on sight. The child was executing on top of the previous game's leftovers - its zero-initialized data, its ARM7's WRAM, the IPC FIFOs, the timers, both CPUs. **What shipped.** `NDS::SetupDownloadPlayBoot` now calls `NDS::Reset()` *before* it writes the image (main RAM, both TCMs, shared WRAM, IPC, timers, DMA, IRQ state, GPU, radio all back to reset), then `MapSharedWRAM(3)`, the FreeBIOS ARM9 logo (`SetupDirectBoot`'s own step), the image, `SPI.GetFirmwareMem()->SetupDirectBoot()` (the firmware header copy and user settings live in main RAM, which the reset had just cleared), the CP15/machine state, the jumps, and finally `Start()` - the sequence `md_load_rom` uses. The ad-hoc `ARM9.Reset()`/`ARM7.Reset()` are gone, subsumed by the real reset. **A real defect in the previous pass's handoff, found by reading the live log.** One frame after `booted the downloaded image` the joiner logged `listening for a download-play beacon`: `step`'s booted gate sat *after* the receive block, and that block **powers the console's radio on**, so the freshly booted client had its radio re-attached and switched on under a game that had just taken it over. The gate is now the first thing `step` checks, the handoff's own turn returns instead of falling through into the handshake send path, and `run` breaks once `dp.is_booted()` rather than spending a driver turn per tick doing nothing. `a_booted_client_stops_touching_its_radio` pins it (it fails if the gate moves back: a booted step would set the bus's receive flag again). **The live result** (`logs/v9sfc_dp_boot7.log.gz`, `logs/v9sfc_dp_route7.log.gz`, route exit 0, nothing set): the transfer is untouched (`2502 distinct packets, 1260340 of the 1260340 bytes`), and the client's log carries **zero** warns and errors of any kind - no undefined instruction, no data abort, no prefetch abort for the whole run. `probe --player 2` samples the ARM9 *inside the downloaded image* on 96-99 of 100 samples (`0201C7B4`, `0201C9E0`, `0201CA40`, `02023F2A`, `02023F4C`), its stack in the DTCM the child itself set up (`027E3B38`), the VBlank IRQ taken (`IME=1`, `IF=0000` after servicing), an ARM7 running the game's own ARM7 code in shared WRAM (`lr7 037F84FC`) that halts in a BIOS SWI between frames, and the child's own game powering its radio on (`WIFI: ON`) with the joiner never touching it again. **What is still not true, and it is the pass's shortfall.** The picture does not advance: the digest is byte-identical over 20 s (`ef647ee79121ad33df5ec9337d8ee562`) while the PC moves on 98-99 of 100 samples, and the child's main display was recorded here as GBATEK's display-off mode (`dispcntA 00011108`, bits 16-17 = 0). **That reading was wrong** (v0.1.9db): those bits are `01` - "graphics display ON" - and melonDS treats only `11` as the display FIFO (`src/GPU.cpp:1084`), so the child was never dark. It made the child look like one that never reached its first frame rather than what it is: a game sitting on its own two-line error box, recorded and decoded in the v0.1.9db row. Two inputs are recorded for the next look: the child's own game switches its radio on and off twice and leaves it off immediately after boot, and it writes three registers melonDS does not implement (`unknown ARM9 IO write32 04001060/64/68`), so its wireless/boot init is where to look next. **No regression:** the complete-transfer result holds exactly and no other title was touched. **Validation.** `cargo test --lib` **37 passed, 0 failed** (one new); `md_smoke_test` **149 checks, 0 failures**; the session quit through the control channel with no strays. **Two build/diagnostic gaps closed on the way.** `src-tauri/build.rs` now watches `src/ARM.cpp`, `src/ARM.h` and `src/CP15.cpp`, so an edit there relinks the host instead of leaving it on a stale core - this pass's own change to `ARM.cpp` silently failed to reach the binary (`cargo build` finished in 0.38 s) until the watch list grew, which is exactly the hazard the file's own comment warns about. And `ARMv5::DataAbort` now logs the address and CPSR it faulted on, not only the PC: the faulting address is the whole question for an ARM9 abort and melonDS was not recording it. The host's own bottom screen carries the *same* first line - x 72..177, 20 characters, the identical glyph pattern, over three lines instead of two - so **both games are in a network-error state, not one**, and the host's picture is only "changing" because its dialog animates. **Why it never advances:** the downloaded title's own wireless init aborts. Its radio comes up at frame 5306 and again at frame 5517 and is powered down within **4 and 1 emulated frames**, each time after writing RF channel values melonDS reports as `invalid channel values 0004C:0004F`; after frame 5518 the client's log is empty for the rest of the session, and `probe` now reads its whole wireless block as idle (`raised none`, `acked none`, `comstatus 0`, `bcn_count 0000 / 0000`). It is not waiting on the link either: `link read_game 0`, `polls 0` - the game has read no link frame and has polled the receive window **zero** times since it booted. Its CPUs are alive and busy: the ARM9's PC moves on 98-99 of 100 samples (sampled inside the 64-bit divide at `0x200857C`, called from the tick-pacing loop `0x2023F28`, which busy-waits on the 64-bit counter at `0x021761B0` until a scaled elapsed time reaches 20), and the ARM7 runs the game's own code out of shared WRAM (`pc7 037FEFDC`, `lr7 03802788`) having pushed **1,573,480** words through the ARM9-facing FIFO. **Two candidate mechanisms are now on the record, both live and neither yet fixed:** (1) the handoff ends the session the downloaded title exists to join - `[dp] handoff: deauthenticated the host (reason 0003)`, the host's game answers by tearing its own access point down (`[mp] USCOMPARECNT disabled: IRQ14 cannot be raised ... this console sends no beacon of its own`) and powering its radio off, and the joiner stops on the `booted` gate - so the client finds no Download Play host to associate with; (2) `Wifi::Reset()` sets `CurChannel = 0`, and `CurChannel == 0` makes a console mute and deaf (`src/Wifi.cpp:1057`, `1339`, `1523` return before the channel byte is stamped; `2738` drops every frame received with `chan != CurChannel || CurChannel == 0`) until the game writes an RF pair the firmware's table contains - which this client never does. Evidence: `logs/v9sfc_dp_wait1.log`, `logs/shots/sfc_wait_resume.png`; helpers `/tmp/glyphs.py` (shot region to glyph cells). |

This fork's boot is a whole-console `NDS::Reset()` (`NDS::SetupDownloadPlayBoot`, v0.1.9da), which sets `CurChannel = 0`, and the child's own bring-up then writes only the firmware's RF register block - values that name no channel (`0004C:0004F`) - so the booted child is tuned nowhere: mute and deaf via `src/Wifi.cpp:1057`/`1339`/`1523` and the `2738` receive drop. `Wifi` gained one held channel (`SetDownloadPlayChannel`/`HeldChannel`): `ChangeChannel()` keeps it whenever the registers name no channel, and a child that *does* select a channel of its own replaces it normally, so the fallback self-disarms. A real client boots *after* the host's Final and continues the association the firmware's Download Play client negotiated (BSSID, AID, role), which is also why a child with a correct channel and no carried association has nothing to attach to. Next: answer the completion round instead of returning before the send path, hold the boot until the host's `05` Final, and carry the association across the boot. **Adversarial notes.** The hold is a fallback, not an override: a child that writes a valid pair switches channels normally, and `HeldChannel` is cleared by `Wifi::Reset()` so no session inherits it; `SetDownloadPlayChannel` rejects out-of-range values (0 or >14) rather than clamping, so a malformed beacon channel leaves the radio untuned instead of tuning it somewhere wrong. **No regression:** the transfer is byte-identical (`2502 distinct packets, 1260340 of the 1260340 bytes`), the handoff still leaves the host's session up, and no other title is on this path. **Validation.** `cargo test --lib` **37 passed, 0 failed** (the hand-off test now also asserts the child keeps the host's channel: `channel == 7`); `md_smoke_test` **149 checks, 0 failures**; the session quit through the control channel with no strays. So the previous pass's reading - the host never sends Final because the completion round went unanswered - is not what is happening: with the round answered and collected, the host's class still goes `04 -> 00` and it never sends Final. **What the host does instead, read live with no pokes.** Its own Download Play engine advances past the download direction and polls Dummy rounds forever: `*(0x020B3DBC)` is still `0x023722E0`, and the per-client mode word at `0x023737C8` (`base + 0x14E8`, the field earlier passes pinned) reads **`00000007`** - it was 6, the download direction - sampled four times over ~25 s, always 7; the gate halfword at `0x02373A34` reads `0000 0005`. The boot must be derived from that transition (or from the host's own acknowledgement of the completed download) rather than from GBATEK's Final, and the transition has to be tested for the deadlock reading: the host holds the association and polls a registered client it never tears down, which is consistent with "I have finished sending, now you" - in which case a Final-gated boot deadlocks by construction. **Adversarial notes.** The boot is idempotent (`!st.booted` plus `handoff`'s own flag). `final_replied` is set only when the reply was actually queued, so the `MD_DP_REPLY_MUTE` diagnostic arm still cannot boot a child it never answered; a Final arriving before the image is complete sets the flag without booting, and a deauth clears the image cleanly. Read and disassembled live, `0x037F852C` is `ldr ip,=0x038043E3 ; bx ip` into `0x038043E2`, which is Thumb `svc 6` - the first entry of the image's own one-instruction-per-SWI stub table (`bx lr`; `svc 7`; ... through `0x0380443E`) - and the same loop is where the **host** sits in the same run (`p1 ... pc7 0000115C lr7 037F84FC`). It is the game's shared ARM7 idle loop, not an error signature, and it does not separate host from child. **The stop, named against a working console.** The host's ladder - `W_ModeWEP <- 004A`, `W_PowerForce <- 8001,0000`, `W_PowerTX <- 0003`, `W_ModeReset <- 0001`, `W_PowerDownCtrl <- 0000`, `W_PowerTX <- 0001`, `W_PowerState <- 0002`, `W_PowerForce <- 8000,0000`, `W_ModeReset <- 0000` - is exactly what raises `TRX power ON`. The child's whole bring-up is `W_ModeWEP <- 003F,001A,0025,0034,0000` (bus walk), `W_PowerUS <- 0000` (`WIFI: ON`), `W_PowerTX <- 0002`, `W_RFCnt <- 0114`, `W_BBMode <- 0100`, `W_ModeWEP <- 0002,000A`, `W_BBPower <- 800D`, `W_PowerUS <- 0001` (`WIFI: OFF`), then `W_PowerUS <- 0000`, `W_BBPower <- 0000,800D`, `W_PowerUS <- 0001` - and nothing more. It never writes `W_PowerState`, `W_PowerForce`, `W_PowerTX`, `W_PowerDownCtrl` or `W_ModeReset`, so it never issues the ladder. The digest counts its wireless block on/off **3/3** (`wireless on/off 3/3, role 2`): it power-cycles the block and gives up, and the game then shows its own `Communication error. / Please turn your DS off.` Everything after that is empty - `W_BBRead` 1018, `W_BBBusy` ~16000, `W_RFBusy` 643, `W_ModeWEP` 11, `W_Random` 6, `W_Preamble` 6 and **zero** reads of `W_IF`/`W_IE`/`W_USCount`/`W_TXStat`/`W_CMDStat`; RX buffer `begin=0000 end=0000`; `USCOUNT=0 USCOMPARE=0`; `role=2` client with no handed frame - where the host spends the same session inside the IRQ/receive loop (`W_IF` 50118, `W_IE` 41493, `W_USCount0` 20681, `W_TXStat` 17508, `W_RFPins` 16293). **And the child is alive, not faulting.** Eight `probe --player 2` samples over ~24 s show `pc9` moving through `0201C790`/`0201C7FC`/`0201CD34`/`02023F2E`, all inside the downloaded image, with `IME=1 IE=2009 IF=0000` and healthy IPC (`7sends 20647 9recvs 20647`, `9sends 103 7recvs 103`); disassembled live, `0201C7F0-0201C81C` is an ordinary function and the ARM9's `02087350` (Thumb) is a game update routine (`add r0,sp,#0xA8 ; bl 0x202EAA4 ; ... ; cmp r0,#10`), not the flag test v0.1.9di hypothesised. **No emulator defect was substantiated, and this is stated rather than dressed up.** Each candidate on the record was checked against the new trace: the busy flags are real and toggle (both consoles' rings interleave `15E:0001/0000`, `180:0001/0000`) and the host works with them; the baseband read path returns the fixed chip id `0x006D` for register 0 and write/read registers round-trip; the channel hold and the carried `W_BSSID*` are present and the child's own writes to them land and read back; IPC, timers and IRQ state are all what the working host requires. Nothing measured is a value the emulator fabricates wrong, so no fix was made - inventing one would have been a change with no evidence behind it. **What the mission's escape clause requires and this pass does not meet.** The stall is localized to the child's own MP-client bring-up, but "inside the child image" is not the same as "outside anything this emulator controls": a wrong emulated value the child rejects would look exactly like this. The instrument that would settle it is a read/store watch on the child's wifi block across its three power cycles that names which read it acts on between the last `W_PowerUS <- 0001` and its first error frame; the ring this run captured holds only busy-flag polls, so that decision is not yet visible. **Validation, with exit statuses.** `cmake --build build-tauri -j8` exit 0 (up to date); `md_smoke_test tools/mkdiagrom/diag.nds` **149 checks, 0 failures, SMOKE_EXIT=0**; `cargo test --lib` **39 passed, 0 failed, CARGO_TEST_EXIT=0**; both routes exit 0 with the transfer byte-identical (2502 distinct packets, 1260340 of 1260340 bytes); session quit through the control channel, app exit status 0, no strays. `logs/v9carry_finding.txt` (new) digests both runs. The host's beacons still flow (1,731 beacon lines after the boot), but not one frame reaches the child (p2 rx frames = 0 for the whole run) and the child sends nothing. **Reading: the obstacle is not the session state, it is the child's own ARM7 bring-up.** The session is genuinely present in the child's `Wifi` core and the host is still transmitting to it, yet the child neither listens nor transmits: its game powers the radio on and off twice more after boot (`WIFI: ON` x2), loads one RF block and never selects a channel or arms a TX slot, leaving the ARM7 in the BIOS idle loop - the same bring-up stop v0.1.9dc/dd/df recorded, now reached on a child that also holds the negotiated association. **Named next blocking step:** what the booted child's ARM7 waits on after its RF block load - at `pc7 0000115C` beside `pc9 0201CD34` - that keeps it from scanning, attaching and arming a TX slot; candidates are the child's own Download Play client init waiting on an ARM9/ARM7 handshake or IRQ its boot never raises, the emulator attachment lifetime (the joiner's bus-level attachment ends at the hand-off and the child's own game never reaches `MP_Begin`), or its power sequence aborting before the channel select. **Adversarial notes.** The carry is gated by the same registry/joiner gate as the rest of the download-play path (no env var, no ROM path - AGENTS.md); Rebuilt as `enum Transfer { Unstated, Streaming, Complete, Collected, Closed }`, advanced in exactly one place (`State::advance_transfer`, a fixpoint walk run once per step after the drain, so one drain carrying both the ACK and the class change still closes that step), reset in exactly one place (the deauthentication arm), logged once per transition, and reported off the control channel (`dpjoiner` -> `transfer`, an atomic holding the enum's discriminant, with `transfer_name` the one owner of the number-to-name mapping). (2) The second run then executed the downloaded ARM9 from `02000850` but faulted at `02008BEC`; the writes were going to the RSA frame's *temp* destinations, and GBATEK says ARM7 in particular is staged at `2Ch` (`022C0000h`) and must end at `30h` (`02380000h`) - fixed by using the actual destinations and also writing the temp ARM7 staging copy. | 2026-10-02 | 0.1.9cy | **The Download Play client's turn now runs inside the console's own frame, so an answer is queued before the host's window opens; the host stops evicting the client and is heard on essentially every round - and its own engine still refuses to start the download, which is now a single, measured fact rather than an inference.** Asked to restructure the answer-delivery path in `dp_joiner`, the joiner bus and the driver servicing in `lib.rs` so an answer is already in the host's queue when its window opens, instead of costing a drain and a send round trip per round, with one owner for the answer schedule and no new env knobs. **The structure, before and after.** Before, the joiner's run loop did everything itself: `step` drained the console's inbox through a queued `LinkOp::Drain`, decided, and then sent each answer through a second queued `LinkOp::SendReply` - two driver turns per round, and a driver turn is one emulated frame, so an answer could only ever land on the frame *after* the window that wanted it. No new env knobs, and no knob was needed to enter the new path - it is the only path. **The effect, same route, nothing poked** (`logs/v9sm64d.log.gz`): `stale` **278 -> 0**, `collected` **31 -> 1711**, and the host's eviction **disappears entirely** - the previous build produced 60,055 of the 6-byte-body no-client command frames after ~2,424 rounds, this one produces **zero** and keeps addressing slave 1 (`slave flags 0002`, 16,434 addressed commands) for the whole run. The rest of the starvation is the transport's retention, now measured: the host asks ~8.6 command rounds per emulated frame and answers arrive in a clump per turn, and the shipped re-anchor keeps one (`MD_REPLY_KEEP=1`); at `MD_REPLY_KEEP=16` (a frame's worth) the host collects **26,968 of 26,977 rounds with `empty 9`, `clientfail 9`, `stale 0`** (`logs/v9sm64e.log.gz`). **The remaining fact, and it is host-owned:** even with the client heard on essentially every round, the host stays at class `00` (RsaDone) and never emits a `04` DataPacket - `download: 0 of 514896 bytes`. With collection healthy the `MD_DP_REPLY_FORCE` sweep is meaningful for the first time and gives the same answer as the starved one: `00` and `09` each park with ~15,700 of ~15,800 rounds collected, `stale 0`, no deauth (`logs/v9sm64f00.log.gz`, `logs/v9sm64f09.log.gz`). Changed: `src-tauri/src/dp_joiner.rs` (`State`/`step` public, `JoinerBus::turn`, the batch answer arm, `run`), `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` (`LinkOp::JoinerTurn`, `DriverTurnBus`, `InstanceJoinerBus::turn`), `compatibility.md`, `history.md`, `to-do.md`. Nothing committed. |

After ~2,424 addressed rounds it evicts the client to the 6-byte-body no-client command form (60,055 frames), `download: 0 of 514896 bytes`, never a `04` - exactly the park the previous pass found, now with its input measured. **The reply's contents are not the cause, and the five-way sweep is the proof:** `MD_DP_REPLY_FORCE` at `09`, `0a`, `0b`, `07` and `00` parks on every value (`logs/sm64_force_sweep.txt`); a `09` forced with the window trace shows the host collecting 21 type-`09` replies and still dropping the client; and the unmodified run already has the host collecting **23 RsaReplies (type `08`)** before it evicts. The starvation is in *when* answers land, not in how many are sent, so the batched path was reverted rather than shipped as unproven churn (`cargo test --lib` back to 33/33 with the tree as it was). `MD_REPLY_BUDGET_FRAMES=20` is worse (`collected 0`, `logs/v9sm64c.log.gz`), and 2,204 of the rounds open with `0us of slack` already. **Next input, named:** the answer has to be in the host's queue *before* its window opens - a drain/send path that does not cost a frame, or a reply window that waits for an answer already on its way - because the host is behaving correctly for a client it cannot hear, and its hearing is the emulator's reply window. **No regression:** Star Fox's whole transfer re-ran on the rebuilt binary (`logs/v9sfc.log.gz`: first packet `#0`, 352 bytes, `"STARFOX "`, then `the download is complete: 2502 distinct packets, 1260340 of the 1260340 bytes the RSA frame stated`). **Validation.** `cargo test --lib` **33 passed, 0 failed**; `md_smoke_test` **149 checks, 0 failures**; every session quit through the control channel (`exited with status 0`, no strays), and the SM64DS run's own save was pinned to a backup before launch. Changed: `compatibility.md`, `history.md`, `to-do.md` (the code was returned to its pre-experiment state by design). Nothing committed. |

Reading it is what made the next step possible but it is not the answer, and the reading is reported as what it was. **The host's DP module is Thumb, and the search for the class ran through it.** Nintendo's Download Play engine is a compact Thumb module at `0x02029C00`-`0x0202A000`: the phase-2 handler at `0x02029DD0` picks a value **`4` or `9`** from `buffer[0x4b2]` and calls the WM send routine at `0x02037D24`, and the phase-5 handler writes `titleA[6] = state+0x0E` and sends `titleA`. Both were held and neither moved the wire class - the live state read `phase 3`, `+0x10 == 9`, `buffer[0x4B2] == 0`, and forcing `buffer[0x4B2] != 0` did change the module's behaviour (`state+0x10` became `4` and the phase machine began cycling `01 -> 03 -> 04`) while the wire stayed class `00`, so `state+0x10` and `titleA[6]` are **not** the wire class and both are recorded as negative measurements. The command body that *is* the wire class was then found by structural search rather than by literal: no code anywhere in ARM9 `0x02000000..0x02400000` contains the literal `0x023722E0`, so the pointer to the body is computed, not loaded from a pool - which is exactly why v0.1.9cp's perturbation of the *records* could never have found it. **The flag, and the proof it is the flag.** The body is the 6-byte block the ARM9 hands the wireless library (`class, 50 08 00 02 00`), read live at `0x023722E0` with its first byte `00`; it is referenced by the TX descriptor table entry at `0x0237A7EC` (`+0x08`) and sits in the WM work area next to the message ring at `0x020B5440` whose `+0x04` points at it with a length of 6 - the ring the ARM9's own IPC pushes name. Forcing byte 0 to `04` and holding it: the joiner's received class becomes `04`, the composer's slot byte becomes `04`, and the wire carries `06 01 02 00 03 11 04 50 08 00 02 00`; letting go, the host restores `00` on its own (`--natural` reads `00`) - so the byte is host-owned live state, rewritten once per round, and the value the host chooses is `00` (`00 Dummy`, GBATEK's "Done/Dummy"), never the data class. **Why it cannot be satisfied from the wire, stated exactly.** The joiner already supplies everything the wire allows: it answers NameRequest with the five username snippets, answers RsaDone, its replies are collected in bursts every round (`our reply collected 22300 times`, `stale 0 deauth out 0`), the association is accepted with `status 0000, AID c001`, and the host logs `[HOST] syncing client C001` - and the class byte is still `00`. The flag is not an input the client sets; it is the *output* of the host's own DP phase decision, written after the host has read its replies, so no frame this client can emit moves it. That is the answer to "why it never selects the data class after RsaDone": the client's side of the handshake is complete and acknowledged, and the class remains the host's own choice. **The patch, labelled.** `tools/scripts/sfc-dp-arm-class.py` writes that one byte through the existing `poke` control command, holds it (`--hold`, default 10 s), reports the classes the joiner saw, restores the original byte, and documents in its own header that this is a **host memory patch, not a client behaviour** - nothing is sent to the host over the link and `dp_joiner.rs` is untouched. It is not a runtime feature and is not wired into the app. **The block layout, captured as the spec for the capture side.** The host's wire frame is `06 01 02 00 | 03 | 11 | CLASS | 50 08 00 02 00` - GBATEK CMD DATA's fixed header `06 01 02 00`, then the six-byte DS header (`03 11` are the size/flags halfword pair the ARM7 library writes from its own descriptor, v0.1.9co), then the class byte the host's DP engine owns, then the host's own five-byte stub body. What is *not* in it, and cannot be until the host's own DP engine builds a payload, is the real data payload GBATEK specifies for class `04` (`09h u16 Packet Number, 0Bh .. data`) - the patched stream carries the host's stub, so the frame layout is real and the packet contents are the host's placeholder. **The honest state of the goal.** The host arms and streams the data command and the joiner collects it, with `md_smoke_test` **141 checks, 0 failures** and `cargo test --lib` **27 passed, 0 failed** after the changes (no emulator source changed this pass - the whole result is a named flag, a labelled patch tool, and captured wire evidence). **Adversarial exercise:** the patcher reads before it writes and restores the exact original three bytes with the class byte overwritten, not the whole word; it refuses to run on a session with no joiner rather than poking blindly; it detects that the byte is host-rewritten and reports hold counts; and its failure mode (never seeing the class) is reported instead of assumed. Validation: `cargo test --lib` **27 passed, 0 failed**; `md_smoke_test` **141 checks, 0 failures**; the session was quit through the app's own channel (`exited with status 0`, no strays). Changed: `tools/scripts/sfc-dp-arm-class.py` (new), `compatibility.md`, `to-do.md`, `BUILD.md`, `history.md`. Nothing committed. |

The joiner answers each command it receives (`pending` = the oldest unanswered command in a drain, not the newest, which the first run got wrong) on a 5 ms tick, re-sending the last class every 250 ms so a missed window is not lost. **A regression this pass introduced and caught live:** a `command == 0x01 if username_idx < 4` guard stopped the *fifth* username snippet from ever being sent, so a live run sat on `NameRequest` forever while collecting snippets 0-3 - the fifth snippet's reply is the one the host needs to leave the name phase, and the guard is gone (covered by `every_name_request_is_answered_with_a_username_even_for_the_last_snippet`). **What the wire now says** (`logs/sfc_reply5.log.gz`, `logs/sfc_rsa2.log.gz`; 2 consoles, Recruitment host on console 1, joiner on console 2, driven by `tools/scripts/sfc-dp-host.sh`): from `status 0000, AID C001` and `[HOST] syncing client C001`, the host's command class advances `01 NameRequest` -> `00 Dummy` -> `03 RSA` (body `06 01 02 00 75 11 03 ...`, size 117 halfwords), every one of the five username snippets is collected (`38 byte frame`, bodies `04 01 07 00/01/02/03/04`), the RSA reply (`04 01 08 ...`) is collected, and the host advances again to `00 Dummy` (RsaDone, GBATEK's name for the class). The joiner's own counters read `host commands 9442 ... our reply collected 711 times`, and the host's digest reads `collected 470 (+61/5s) ... stale 0 reanchor-dropped 0 deauth out 0`. **Where it now sits, named.** After RsaDone the host sends no `04 DataPacket`, and the reason is outside the protocol: its Recruitment lobby is a UI screen ("Searching for opponents ... Touch the Cut Off button to move on. After that tap the host leaves the lobby onto its "Please wait a moment..." screen and holds at command `00` for minutes with no `04` - so the next command class is gated by a host-side button, not by another frame from the client. **Adversarial exercise:** the truncated-command guard, the batch ordering (the oldest unanswered command, not the newest), the ack-driven snippet advancement, and the fifth-snippet regression each have a focused unit test; the disarmed joiner still touches the bus zero times; the reply timestamp's staleness budget is the host's own constant.
