| 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9cl | **The Star Fox host now accepts the client: it registers it in its own game (`[HOST] syncing client C001`), arms its command window and begins the Download Play handshake - and the untested link the mission named was real, but not the AID the client sends: the host had never assigned one, because the client's association-request SSID was missing the two bytes every captured real client echoes from the beacon's `DD` tag.** Asked to test the host-assigned AID against what the joiner carries by reading the host's association-response body and its slave/AID state directly, then make the joiner carry what the host assigned. **The response body was never read, and it said `refused`.** The link trace's `sync=` field is the frame body's first eight bytes as one little-endian u64 (`PlatformBridge.cpp` `trace_frame`: `sync = *(u64*)&frame[24]`), so an association response decodes in place: a working host's body reads `sync=0201C00100000021` = capability `0021`, status `0000`, **AID `C001`**, rates `01 02`, identical to `WifiAP`'s own builder - while every Star Fox joiner run read `sync=0201000000010021` = status **`0001`** (unspecified failure), **AID `0000`**. Corroborated independently by the host's own join path: `[HOST] syncing client` fires in every captured real-game log (`mph4b`: `syncing client C001`/`C002`) and never once in a Star Fox joiner run, and that line (`src/Wifi.cpp` ~1633) requires a nonzero AID - so the host was refusing, not accepting, and the previous record's "host accepts the association" was a misread of a field nothing decoded. The joiner could not have known either: its `Rx::AssocResponse` was a unit variant that dropped the body. **The field that was actually missing.** Real game clients' association-request SSIDs, byte for byte from the captures, are `serial(4) + <beacon DD bytes 16..18>(2) + 00 00 + name/padding(24)`: MPH's client sends `0A 00 40 00 12 23 00 00` (`logs/mph4b_app_4con.log.gz`) against a beacon whose `DD` tag carries `... 0A 00 40 00 12 23 54 01`; NSMB's sends `50 01 40 00 DA A3 00 00` (`logs/nsmb_app.log.gz`) against `... 50 01 40 00 DA A3 1E 01`; a third title's sends `25 00 00 00 A9 D4 00 00` against `... 25 00 00 00 A9 D4 1C 01`. Three independent titles, same rule: the client echoes the beacon's `DD` bytes 12..18 and zeroes the last two of its first word. Our joiner echoed only the four-byte serial and left the next two zero (`18 02 40 00 00 00 00 00`), which is the one field the host was refusing on. **The fix, and what it changed.** `HostBeacon` gained `token` (the beacon's `DD` bytes 16..18), `client_ssid(serial, token)` builds the real client's shape, and `Rx::AssocResponse { status, aid }` now carries the two fields that decide the join; the acceptance test is **status 0 *with* a nonzero AID** - the same condition the host's own `[HOST] syncing client` and `IsMPClient` make - so a refusal can no longer be logged as an acceptance, and no download handshake starts on one. **Measured live, twice** (`logs/sfc_dp_token.log.gz`, `logs/sfc_dp_token2.log.gz`; 2 consoles, Star Fox Recruitment host on console 1, joiner on console 2, driven by `tools/scripts/sfc-dp-host.sh`): each run is one clean handshake - `auth sent 1 / recv 1`, `assoc sent 1 / recv 1`, `last assoc status 0x0000, AID 0xc001` - and the host log now carries `[HOST] syncing client C001`, its command slot arms (`slots beacon 811C cmd 8000`), and it transmits `fc=0228` commands whose body decodes (GBATEK CMD layout) to reply-time `0106`, **slave flags `0002` (slave 1 connected)**, size 3 and command `01` = **NameRequest** - the download handshake, started by the host's own game. **Where it now sits, named from both counters.** The host repeats that NameRequest for the whole session (13,614 commands in run 1) while its reply window collects nothing (`collected 0`, `clientfail` growing every window), because the joiner's answers leave as broadcast packets through `md_link_send_packet` -> `MP_SendPacket`, which is the regular receive path, not the reply path the host reads through `Platform::MP_RecvReplies` -> `LocalMP::RecvReplies` (keyed by AID). No bridge exposes the reply send yet (`md_link_send_packet` is the only link-send surface; the core has `MP_SendReply`). So the next step is concrete: answer the NameRequest from the reply path with the AID the host assigned (`C001` -> aid 1), and the reply body is the next thing to reconstruct from captures. **Adversarial exercise:** the acceptance predicate is tested against both the observed refusal (`status 1, aid 0`) and a working grant (`status 0, aid 0xC001`), and the SSID builder against MPH's and NSMB's captured bytes; the disarmed path is still zero bus operations and a non-Download-Play title still gets no thread. Validation: `cargo test --lib` **20 passed, 0 failed** (new: the refusal is not an acceptance and starts no handshake, the `DD` token is parsed and echoed); `cargo build --release` clean; two live two-console sessions driven and quit through the app's own control channel, no strays, no `Test Roms` save touched (Star Fox has none). The joiner's one-time beacon log now also carries the parsed `token`, capability, rates and SSID, so a future title's `DD` layout is read off a live run. Changed: `src-tauri/src/dp_joiner.rs`, `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`, `tools/appctl.py`, `compatibility.md`, `to-do.md`, `history.md`. Nothing committed. |
| 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9ck | **The client is made as faithful as the captures allow - it now echoes the host's advertised game serial in its SSID and sends the real first post-association frame - and the host still deauths, so the previous pass's premise is disproved with evidence: `cmds rx` can never be non-zero on a host, a command frame reaches the host's game and does not unblock it, and the host's own game never arms its command slot, so the download never begins.** Asked to reconstruct a real client's first command from captured traffic, send it after association, and either see the host's deauth stop and the download handshake start or name the frame the host refuses. **What the captures actually say.** GBATEK's Download Play chapter settles the direction: commands are *host-to-client* (`DA = 03:09:BF:00:00:00`, `W_TXBUF_CMD`, multiplayer master) and clients send *replies* (`DA = 03:09:BF:00:00:10`, `W_TXBUF_REPLY1`, slave); a repo-wide scan of every captured multi-card log confirms it - the only console that ever writes a command is the host (`p1 tx cmd` in every session; the one apparent exception, `nsmb_app.log.gz`, is a session where roles were swapped and p2 *was* the host). So the frame to reconstruct is the client's first reply, and it is in the captures: `logs/mph4b_app_4con.log.gz` has the client's own `tx frame: slot 5 (REPLY) fc=0118 len=30 seq=0020`, its body `00 00` (a short dummy reply; the host logs the same frame cropped as `26 byte frame, body[2]=00 00`), addressed `rx=<host> tx=<client> bssid=0309BF000010`. `dp_joiner.rs` now builds exactly that (`build_dummy_reply`) and sends it once the association is accepted. **A real defect the beacon named.** Every captured host's beacon carries its game serial in the proprietary `DD` tag at data offset 12 (`logs/*_app.log.gz`): MPH `0A 00 40 00`, NSMB `50 01 40 00`, Star Fox `18 02 40 00`; and every one of those hosts' *clients* put the very same word at the head of their 32-byte SSID. Our joiner had been sending `25 00 00 00` - a word borrowed from an unrelated capture - so it was asking for a title the host did not have. It is now parsed out of the beacon (`HostBeacon.serial`, from the `DD` tag) and echoed (`client_ssid(serial)`, verified in the live log: `[dp] hearing ... game serial 00400218`, and the association body reads `21 00 01 00 00 20 18 02 40 00 ...`). **The premise that does not survive contact.** The previous pass inferred from the host's digest `cmds rx 0` that it was waiting for a client command. It cannot be: `MPD.cmds_rx` is incremented only in CheckRX's `else if (macgood && IsMPClient)` branch (`src/Wifi.cpp` ~2860), so a console that is not an MP *client* can never increment it - `cmds rx 0` is the only value a host can print. A probe that sent a command frame (`fc=0228`) to the multicast command MAC confirmed it changes nothing: the host's game received it (`packet delivered to the console's own software: 36 byte frame, fc=0228`), and it was also dropped by the host's own programmed filter (`cmd seq=... dropped before any handling: RXFilter2 kills from/to=2 (RXFilter2=000D)`), and the host neither answered nor stopped deauthenticating. **What the host does with what we send.** The real dummy reply reaches the host's game and is *consumed* (`handed: len=38 fc=0118 ... consumed=26 halfwords`, the read order ending on the body's two bytes), the association is accepted every time (3/3 in `logs/sfc_dp_final.log.gz`), and the host's game never writes its command slot at all - `slots beacon 811C cmd 0000 reply1 0000 reply2 0000` on every `tx frame`, `W_TXSlotCmd` never armed, `cmds tx 0`. Its ARM9 sits in the same idle loop (`arm9pc 0x02030488`) and it deauthenticates (`reason 0001`, 30-byte frame, 6-byte body) ~3.9s after each association, then re-accepts. So the blocker is not the client's post-association frame: the host's own Download Play code never starts the transfer, and neither the client's real first frame nor any frame class we can send from the wire reaches the condition it waits on. **Validation.** `cargo test --lib` **18 passed, 0 failed** (new: the `DD`-tag serial is parsed and echoed, the dummy reply's shape and 30-byte length); `cargo build --release` clean; two live two-console sessions driven and quit through the app's own control channel, no strays. Removed after it earned nothing: the command-frame probe and its counter (the code's own rule - complexity for a risk that turned out not to be real). Changed: `src-tauri/src/dp_joiner.rs`, `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`, `tools/appctl.py`, `history.md`, `compatibility.md`, `to-do.md`. Nothing committed. |
| 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9cj | **The synthesized Download Play client now gets in: the host grants its authentication (`status 0000`) and answers its association with an `fc=0010` response, and the user space then names the exact frame the host waits on next - a client command frame addressed to the multicast `03:09:BF:00:00:00`, which never comes, after which the host deauthenticates (reason `0001`, 6-byte body) and re-accepts, about every 4 s.** Asked to fix the joiner's authentication so the host grants it, then follow the association through and name whatever the host asks next, measuring every step from the wire. **Root cause of the old `status 000E`.** The frame builders omitted the hardware's 4-byte FCS slot, so `LocalMP::ApplyWireFCS` wrote the CRC over the last four bytes of the body and the host read its transaction sequence number out of a checksum (GBATEK `W_TXBUF_LOCn[0Ah]`: the TX length includes the FCS the hardware generates). With the slot restored our authentication traces `len=46 fc=00B0 seq=0000` (a 34-byte frame: `24 header + 6 body + 4 FCS`), exactly a real client's, and the host answers `sequence 0002, status 0000`. **This pass's fix: make the association byte-for-byte a real client's.** A captured DS client's association request (`logs/ab_ctl_app.log.gz`, `body[0..41]` / `tx frame: slot 2 (LOC2) fc=0000 len=70 seq=0010`) is `capability(2) + listen interval(2) + SSID IE(2+32) + the four supported-rate bytes raw` - **no rates information element**; Nintendo's own layout (`WifiAP::RecvPacket`) puts four raw rate bytes after the fixed fields and the IEs after those. Our builder was sending an `01 04 01 02 82 84` rates IE (72 bytes on the air) and echoing the host's empty SSID; it now sends the client's own 32-byte SSID element (`25 00 00 00` + name) and the four raw rate bytes, so the request is **70 bytes** and traces `len=82 fc=0000 seq=0010 sync=0025200000010021` - identical to the captured client. `parse_beacon` was corrected the same way (it had logged "rates 2 bytes" out of a beacon that carried four): the rates are the four raw bytes at body `12..16`, and the `parse_ies` scan for the SSID starts at body 16 and no longer looks for a rates element. **The measurement** (`logs/sfc_dp_join3.log.gz`, 2 consoles, Star Fox Recruitment host, joiner on console 2): **47 authentications, 47 answers, every one `status 0000`**; **47 associations sent, 46 answered with `fc=0010`** (`p1 tx len=50 fc=0010 rx=0009BF126643 ... sync=0201000000010021`); and **46 host deauthentications `reason 0001`**. So the host *accepts* the joiner and the download conversation is entered, then the host stalls and retries. **The exact frame it waits on, named from the host's own counters.** An MP "command" is defined at `src/Wifi.cpp:2006` as a frame whose address-1 equals `MPCmdMAC = 03:09:BF:00:00:00` (multicast). The host's digest reads `cmds rx 0` continuously while its game polls the receive window (`window reads 3700 (+74/cycle)`, `polls 9869 (empty 9865)`), it hands our association to its game marked `fc=0000 ... (not a command)`, and ~3.9 s later it emits `deauth sent: ... 30 byte frame, reason 0001 (body is not the usual 2-byte reason code)` and re-accepts the next association. So the download handshake's first missing piece is a **client command frame addressed to `03:09:BF:00:00:00`**; the association already reached the game's hands, and the deauth is the host's timeout for the command that follows it, not a refusal of the association. **Adversarial exercise:** the builders are pure and unit-tested against captured bytes; the disarmed path is still zero-bus-ops and a title without `download_play` still gets no thread; the beacon parser's new layout was checked against `WifiAP`'s own beacon builder. Validation: `cargo test --lib` **16 passed, 0 failed** (new: the association's shape and its 70-byte length, the beacon layout); `cargo build --release` clean; the two-console run driven and quit through the app's own control channel, no strays. Changed: `src-tauri/src/dp_joiner.rs`, `history.md`, `compatibility.md`, `to-do.md`. Nothing committed. |
| 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9ci | **The Download Play client's first half is built, gated to one title by the registry rather than an env var, and the Star Fox host answers it - with a refusal it states itself: its authentication response carries status `000E` ("unexpected transaction sequence number") and it deauthenticates the association with reason `0006` ("class-2 frame from nonauthenticated station").** Asked to implement the joiner half far enough to provoke the host using the captured beacons as reference, keep it strictly gated so every other title's link path stays byte-identical when inactive, prove that gating rather than assert it, and measure with the link tracer. **What was built, and where.** `src-tauri/src/dp_joiner.rs` is the whole client: a pure, unit-tested parser for the host's `fc=0080` beacon (MAC, BSSID, channel, capability, SSID/rates IEs) and builders for the two frames a client sends first - an open-system authentication request, then an association request echoing the beacon's own capability, SSID and rates. A background thread drains one console's inbox, classifies what it finds, and answers a beacon. It goes through the existing bus (`md_link_send_packet` on the console's own driver thread), so no emulator core changed; the one new bridge surface is `md_console_mac` (the joiner addresses frames as its own console). **Gating is the registry.** `game_profile.rs` gained an `ASFE` entry with a `download_play` flag, and `GameProfile::download_play_joiner()` is the single predicate - title is Download Play *and* the one Settings switch is on. Every other title gets no thread; the switch reaches a running session through `set_per_game_fix`. **Three defects found by running it, not by reading it.** (1) The first live run heard **nothing**: a console whose radio is off is not *attached* to the bus, so the router never wrote it the host's beacons - the client has to attach at the bus level, which is the existing `md_link_test_attach` semantics. (2) Even then it heard nothing: Star Fox's own boot-time wireless power cycle (`MP_Begin`/`MP_End`) calls `End`, detaching the console underneath the attach, so a new `md_link_attached` bridge read exists and the attachment is re-asserted rather than assumed. (3) The unit tests caught the ordinary mistakes (a mock's mutability, the deauth-restart path). **What the wire shows** (`logs/sfc_dp_join.log.gz`, two consoles, console 1 in Recruitment, joiner on console 2; `md-link.py census`): the host's game reads **every** joiner frame (205 auth requests, 205 associations, `dest: unicast`), answers the authentication **205/205** times (`fc=00B0` to `0009BF126643`, body `algorithm 0000 sequence 0002 status 000E`, traced as `sync=5A5A000E00020000`), and answers the association **zero** times: its reply is a `fc=00C0` deauthentication with `reason 0006` (`p1 deauth 0009BF112233 -> 0009BF126643 reason 0006 x205`). So the first frame the host *refuses* is now named, with the host's own status and reason codes, and the next client step is the authentication transaction it expects rather than the association. **Gating proven, not asserted.** Same route, Settings switch off before the host walk: `beacons 0 · auth sent 0 / recv 0 · assoc sent 0 / recv 0`, **zero frames from the joiner console on the wire** while the host beacons normally (`logs/sfc_dp_off.log.gz`); and on New Super Mario Bros. `dpjoiner` answers `this title has no download-play joiner` - no thread exists (`logs/nsmb_dp.log.gz`). **Validation.** `md_smoke_test` **141 checks, 0 failures** (three new: console MAC readable/distinct, a never-booted console refuses a MAC, bus attachment readable and reversible); `cargo test --lib` **15 passed** (beacon parse, both frame builders byte-exact, gate on/off, deauth classification and client restart, malformed-frame rejection); `cmake --build` and `cargo build --release` clean; three live two-console sessions driven through the app's own control channel and quit through it, no strays. New tooling: `tools/appctl.py dpjoiner`. New bridge surface: `md_console_mac`, `md_link_attached`, `LocalMP::IsAttached`. Changed: `src-tauri/src/dp_joiner.rs` (new), `src-tauri/src/game_profile.rs`, `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`, `src/net/LocalMP.{h,cpp}`, `src/frontend/tauri/{Bridge.cpp,melonds_bridge.h}`, `tools/appctl.py`, `tools/coretest/smoke_test.cpp`, `to-do.md`, `compatibility.md`, `history.md`. `src/Wifi.cpp` is untouched. | 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9ch | **The cheap Download Play path was tested first and it stops one step short of the app: there is no DS firmware blob to boot, and the project cannot ship one - so the firmware menu (which *is* the Download Play client) is unreachable on this machine.** Asked to bring up the Star Fox Recruitment host via the v0.1.9cg driver and alongside it a console booted into the firmware menu with no cartridge, implementing minimal per-console support if the app cannot do that, and to judge success from state rather than pictures. **What was checked, and what it settled.** A filesystem sweep finds no `firmware.bin`/`bios7.bin`/`bios9.bin` anywhere under `$HOME` and none in the repo (the only `.bin` files are debug dumps under `logs/`), and the app's own data dir `~/.melonDS-multiplayer` does not exist. melonDS cannot stand in for the blob: `Firmware::IsBootable()` returns false for its generated firmware (`src/SPI_Firmware.cpp:345`, rejecting `GENERATED_FIRMWARE_IDENTIFIER`), and `NDS::NeedsDirectBoot()` (`src/NDS.cpp:280`) returns true unless a *bootable* firmware plus known-native BIOS are loaded - so BIOS blobs alone do not produce a menu either. The app is missing the path too, but that is downstream of the blob: it requires a cartridge for a session at all (`MD_AUTOSTART_PLAYERS` with no `MD_AUTOSTART_ROM` logs `skipping autostart` and starts **no** console - measured, `logs/sfc_norom.log`), it loads the **same** ROM into every console (`src-tauri/src/lib.rs:1138`), and `md_set_firmware`/`md_set_bios` have **no callers** anywhere in `src/` or `src-tauri/`, so no command or env var can reach a firmware image even if one existed. **What minimal per-console support would take, stated rather than written:** a per-console boot spec (cartridge / no cartridge), a firmware path wired through to `md_set_firmware`, and skipping `SetupDirectBoot` when a bootable firmware is present. It is straightforward but untestable on any machine without the blob, so no speculative plumbing was added - the mission's own done-condition allows "a precise account of the exact point it stops with the evidence that proves it", and the stopping point is the firmware image itself, not the app. **Consequence for the plan:** Route A cannot be exercised here at all, which settles that the built-in client (Route B) is not merely preferred but the only implementable route in this checkout. No source changed this pass. Validation: app launched with players and no ROM and quit through its own channel, no strays; the v0.1.9cg driver and its captured wire from the previous pass are unchanged. Changed: `to-do.md`, `history.md`. Nothing committed. |
| 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9cg | **Star Fox Command's Download Play host is now reachable from one command, and the question that decides the feature's shape is answered with file-and-line evidence: the receive-side multiboot path does not exist, here or upstream, so the client must be written from scratch.** Asked to add a reusable driver under `tools/scripts` that takes Star Fox from a cold two-console launch into its Download Play / Recruitment screen deciding progress from emulator and link state (the ASCII readers had already failed on this font), run it to a reproducible unattended success, record the wire, and settle whether any receive-side download path exists under another name. **The driver.** `tools/scripts/sfc-dp-host.sh` measures the touch geometry once and then never reads text: the title screen advances on any touch (START does nothing), the main menu's four panels have separators at y 57/109/161 with Download Play as panel 2, and the Download Play page's own option spans y ~51..141. All three touch targets contain (128, 96), so the walk is one tap repeated and self-correcting - a tap that lands during a transition costs a retry, not a wrong path. Success is link state only: `role=1`, this console's bit on the bus, and 204-byte `fc=0080` beacons leaving. Two cold runs pass from a single command (`PASS: console 1 is the Recruitment host, beacons on the wire (23, +23)`, `logs/sfc_dp_host.log`). **A real defect in the first version, found by running it and fixed:** `beacons()` used `grep -c ... || echo 0`, and `grep -c` prints `0` *and* exits 1 when it matches nothing, so the counter emitted two lines and the verdict's arithmetic broke (`[: 0\n0: integer expression expected`); it now captures the count into a variable and prints one integer. **What the wire actually is.** `md-link.py census` over the captured session (`logs/sfc_dp_wire.log`) shows the host writes **only** beacons: 42 frames of `fc=0080`, 204 bytes, broadcast to `FFFFFFFFFF`, into TX slot 4, and the peer's game reads 0 of them because it is not in Download Play. No command, reply or download class appears. **This corrects the roadmap's premise:** the earlier "complete bidirectional exchange" (`sent=489 (99,532 B)` / `received=501 (101,980 B)`, `logs/rom_sfc_harness.log`) is not a host serving a child image - both consoles logged as `[host]`, so there was never a client and the download conversation has never been observed. The client's protocol is unknown, not merely unimplemented. **The receive-path question, answered.** Repo-wide keyword sweeps find no multiboot/download-play/child-image code in this tree or in `upstream/master`; `NDS::SetupDirectBoot()` copies from `NDSCartSlot.GetCart()->GetROM()` (`src/NDS.cpp:297`; upstream identical at `src/NDS.cpp:297`), i.e. it boots the cartridge and never a received image; and the only `MB_*` symbols in either tree are `src/DSi_NWifi.h:89`'s heartbeat Mailbox (`Mailbox[n].Read()`), the DSi wifi service IPC, not DS multiboot. So the joiner must boot the cartridge today and the client must be written from scratch. No emulator source changed this pass. Validation: driver `bash -n` clean and run cold twice (exit 0 both times); app quit through its own control channel; no strays; Star Fox has no `.sav`. The link is not what ended it: 640 acks, 0 of them reporting a failed client, and the beacon slot is still armed`. Both guests' own games then deauth (`reason 0003`, `p2 0009BF126643 -> 0009BF112233`, `p3 0009BF13AA53 -> 0009BF112233`) and power their radios off; the host's roster never lists a second player (`pill 0`) (`logs/mp3c_app.log`, `logs/mp3c_match.log`). **The instruments exonerate the link.** Run trace-verbose: `md-link.py census` shows the host wrote 640 CMD + 640 ACK, **every frame a console wrote reached its peer's game** (`frames the fork itself did not carry: none`), both guests received all 640 commands (p2 640 direct, p3 426 addressed + 214 group), and each answered from its own arm; the host's reply windows read `has everyone it asked for` and its acks report `clientfail 0`; the host addressed both clients (`mask 0x0006`) for 426 rounds. `md-link.py rounds` pairs guest answers to host collects as **identical 1065, DIFFERENT 0**. Group addressing, per-client reply, ordering and the join-time mask all work for the extra console; the stop is MPH's own host game and is unchanged by what the link hands it. **Verdict: the mission's second branch - MPH's own code - so no link code changed and every other title's path is untouched.** One confound removed: the three-console runs used `MD_KICK_AFTER_WINDOWS=0` because the stall-kick watchdog resyncs a console during multi-guest joins (its documented hazard), but the default-watchdog run fails identically (the host is kicked three times pre-join, the same guest deauths follow), so the kick is not the trigger. | 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9ca | **The park's frame classes are counted at both ends and every class the games write is carried - and at a longer park than any earlier sample both ARM9s are halted in the ARM9 BIOS's own wait loop with the dequeue's queue empty, so the wall is not a frame class the fork fails to route.** Asked to test the one hypothesis every byte-level view so far could not reach - a class of frame that is never carried at all (multicast or group-addressed frames, an un-routed management frame, a slot the games write that nothing forwards) - by first naming the message, flag or payload value each game is blocked on from its own ARM9 wait path, then answering *with counts* whether the link carries that class between the two consoles. **The instrument.** `md-link.py census` (new subcommand, `--park-us N`, `--end-us`) counts the same session at two independent interfaces instead of comparing bytes: the *writer* (`[mp] tx frame`: which of the six TX slots each game's own frame left, with its fc) and the *carrier* (`[link-trace]`: what each console put on the air and what each console's game read off it, with the destination address's kind), classed by frame control rather than by the API that moved it - one API carries several classes here (the guest's own game reads the host's commands, its acks *and* its beacons through `MP_RecvHostPacket`), which is exactly the blur that hid the question. It also counts every frame the fork itself refused (bad length, channel mismatch, unusual slot bits) and the guest's arm writes per slot, and prints the games' own closing deauths separately, since those *are* carried. **Tested adversarially before it was trusted:** a synthetic log with five command writes and no read shows the class unmatched (`p1 wrote 5 -> p2's game read 0 (+5)`), a synthetic `bad frame length` line is counted under the fork's own gate, an empty log says so instead of blaming the games for empty slots, a missing file exits with the tool's message, and `--park-us` on a log with no frames says there is nothing to count. **The counts.** Fresh two-console run of the documented route (`--restart --no-dialogs --players 2 --cut-off --vs --watch 40`, `logs/v9f0`), whole session 62,818 commands: CMD 62,818 written by p1 / 62,818 read by p2, ACK 62,818/62,818, REPLY 62,817 written / 62,815 collected (+2), BEACON 4,800 written / 4,673 read (+127), AUTH 1+1, ASSOC-REQ 1, ASSOC-RESP 1, one `fc=0158` firmware stand-in that was never read, and **no refusal of any kind** (`frames_dropped` stayed 0; no `bad frame length`, no channel mismatch). Slot 0 was never written by either game's own code in the whole session and slots 2/3 only for the four association frames and the two closing deauths, so there is no class the games write that nothing forwards. The 127 beacons (2.6 %) are the only unmatched traffic anywhere, and they are not a lost class either: 125 of them were written before the guest's radio ever read one (the host's beacons from its own `wireless ON` at t=37.3M us; the guest's first beacon read is at t=65.2M us), a shape the two recorded park runs repeat (`logs/v9e0`: 126 of 127 missing before the first read; `logs/v9d1`: 130) - the pre-coupling stretch, not a refused frame. Group-addressed destinations are carried: commands and acks to `03:09:BF:00:00:00` / `03:09:BF:00:00:03`, beacons to `FF:FF:FF:FF:FF:FF`, every one of them read at the peer. **The parked window** (the last 60 emulated seconds before the games gave up, `--park-us 60000000 --end-us 1108200000`, `logs/v9f0_census_park.txt`) is a perfect steady state: CMD 3,590/3,590, ACK 3,590/3,590, REPLY 3,591/3,590, BEACON 269/269. **The named wait, live.** `logs/v9f0_park_pcs.txt`: both ARM9s are in the **ARM9 BIOS's own wait loop** (`pc9 0xFFFF0238`, the `SWI Halt` WFI sequence - 37/40 samples on p1 and 40/40 on p2, the remaining samples brief excursions through the game's own poll handler around `0x0212F4E8`), the wifi block reads `W_IF 88C0`/`0800` with **`W_IE 0000` on both**, and the queue the v0.1.9as dequeue spins on is **empty on both consoles** (`0x2173300` producer == `0x21732AC` consumer: `0x4B/0x4B` on p1, `0x49/0x49` on p2, read live through `appctl.py mem`). So the correction to the v0.1.9as reading is measured, not argued: at this park the console is *not* inside the blocking dequeue - it is halted with nothing queued, and `W_IE 0` means the radio could not raise the wake-up that would release it. The payloads are frozen in the same shape: the last 6,000 command-body views (3,000 rounds, printed once per console per new seq) are byte-identical, `BE 01 02 00 00 80`, and the guest's own answer takes exactly two forms in its last 6,000 replies - `00 00 00 00 B8 B6` 4,584 and `00 80 00 00 B8 B6` 1,416 - so **one alternating bit is the only thing either game varies**, round after round. **The run itself** held the pre-race wait for ~1,039 emulated seconds (frames 4,469 to ~66,308) and ended the way the record says it does: the guest's own `reason 0003` deauth first (`t=1,108,287,136`), the host's 223,521 us (13 emulated frames) later, `link mask 0x3` throughout. **Verdict: the done-condition's second branch** - the fork demonstrably carries every class the two games exchange, counted both ways, and the count also names the waits (an empty queue under a WFI-halted CPU with the block's interrupts cleared, and payloads that never change except one bit). | 2026-10-01 | 0.1.9bt | **The MKDS asymmetry is settled in bytes and the fork is not carrying the guest a stale or duplicated body: every reply the host collected is exactly what the guest's own game wrote (4,750 + 6,150 rounds, 0 differences), every command the guest's game received was a new seqno (4,756 of 4,756), the frame lengths carry the wire's `+4` FCS crop, and the guest's own game keeps arming, answering and reading right through the window its picture froze in (arm serial `1 -> 4755` monotonic, 4,700 answers written after the tap) - so the guest is stalling inside its own game, not in the emulation.** Asked to settle with bytes whether the fork feeds the guest a stale or duplicated body, taking one round at a time across the alternation and comparing what the guest's own game writes to its reply slot (and what the host's game writes to its command slot) against the bytes the fork actually carries, then to fix the fork if they disagree and otherwise to prove the guest is stalling in its own game by showing its own clocks and slot writes still advancing. **The instrument** is `tools/scripts/mkds-round-bytes.py`: it pairs the verbose log's five views of one round - the guest's own reply-slot write (Wifi.cpp `TXSendFrame` case 5, recorded from that console's own TXBuffer), the arm it came from, the host's own CMD-slot write (the link trace's `cmd body`), the bytes the fork carried (LocalMP's per-client buffer), what the host's game was handed, and the guest's own `cmd from host` record - by frame sequence **in log order**. The pairing is the whole trick and the instrument got it wrong twice before it got it right: keyed on the 12-bit seq in a dictionary, a long run reuses every value and the first answer pairs with the last collection, which reported **4,720 "DIFFERENT" rounds on a faithful link**; and the two `cmd body` prints per frame are the sender's and the receiver's copy (PlatformBridge's dedupe is per `(seq, slot)` and the slot byte differs between the two boxes), so taking the second attributed the guest's own receive buffer to the host. Both are recorded in the code now. A third correction was to the comparison itself: the wire owns the frame's trailing four bytes, so the writer's buffer still holds whatever the game left there (`5A 5A B8 B6` for the lobby template, replaced by the computed CRC), and comparing the raw bodies reads `00 80 5A 5A B8 B6` against `00 80 AE 2C C6 31` for what is in fact a byte-faithful frame - that slot has to be excluded or every round looks wrong. **The verdict, on two runs.** *(1) guest -> host:* 4,750 rounds in `logs/v9an_*` and 6,150 in `logs/v9ai_*`, **all identical, none different** - every reply the host's game collected carries exactly the bytes the guest's own game wrote. *(2) host -> guest:* 4,590 and 5,287 rounds identical; **all 4,756 commands the guest's game received are the guest's own record of `a new command, delivered` and not one is `the same seqno as the last command`**, against 3,684 distinct command seqs the host wrote - so no duplicate and no stale command was ever fed. *(3)* The frame lengths agree at the exact `+4` of the wire's FCS crop in 4,613 of 4,756 rounds (the outliers are the join's few long bodies). **Who wrote the alternating bodies** is answered by (1) and by where they are read: the 49 distinct bodies the guest's slot write carried in the fresh run (`00 80 03 00 B8 B6` 1,448 times, `00 80 04 00 B8 B6` 1,427, `00 80 5A 5A B8 B6` 661, and their `00 00` siblings) come out of the **guest's own TX buffer**, so the alternation is its own game's output and not the carrier's. **Is the guest's own game still running in the frozen window?** Yes, and by its own numbers: it armed and wrote its reply slot 4,755 times with the arm serial monotonic `1 -> 4755` and its own wireless clock monotonic `68347282 -> 132666600`; 4,700 of those answers were written and 4,695 collected **after** the CUT OFF tap at frame 4,144; and its game read 28,542 halfwords out of the 3,684 commands it was handed. **Residual, recorded and not fixed:** 5 rounds in each run (<0.15 %) where the trace's view of the frame the guest's game reads at payload offset 24 holds `A5 A5` (stale buffer content) instead of the host's bytes, while those rounds' lengths are the healthy `+4` and the guest's answers in them still move; the trace's own comment says the write cursor can advance by a halfword between frames, and on a 0.1 % discrepancy this instrument cannot attribute there is nothing to fix without changing the receive path on a misread - the mission's rule. **So the fork is exonerated for this asymmetry and no production code changed**, and the half of the done-condition that needed a re-run with the guest's picture advancing again is **not met**: nothing on our side moves the guest's state machine, which is where the evidence now points. | 2026-09-30 | 0.1.9bo | **The app's "Link test" no longer reports FAILED on a healthy bus, and the failure was never the receive path: the button waited for a byte the wire overwrites.** Its test frame carried its nonce in the frame's *trailing four bytes*, and those are the hardware's FCS slot - `SendPacketGeneric` writes the 802.11 checksum *into the frame* there (now one implementation, `LocalMP::ApplyWireFCS`, used by the sender and by the test's expectation alike, GBATEK `W_TXBUF_LOCn[0Ah]`) - so the arriving frame's last word was always the checksum (0x00000000 for a 16-byte frame's empty body; pinned in the smoke test against zlib's CRC-32), and the old matcher `got[3] == nonce` could not be true in *any* state. The receive total is now attributed instead of guessed: `MDLinkStats` v3 adds the router's own ledger `packets_delivered` (counted in `LocalMP::Route` at the hand-over, before any consumer can touch it) beside `packets_read_game` / `packets_read_drained`, and the health line prints all three - in the 4-console diag state, the line that used to read `rx 9(+9)` now reads `[game 0(+0), drain 9] · delivered 9(+9)`, i.e. the "healthy bus" the old test was contradicting *was the test's own drains*. The button now arms an expectation on the receiver (`md_probe_arm/disarm/result`) and asks two independent questions: the ledger moved, and some consumer - the console's own game or our drain - read those exact bytes back with the timestamp they were queued with (`seenBy` names which). **The new instrument diagnosed its own mirror first**: the Rust copy of `MDLinkStats` had the v3 block before `bytes_sent` rather than after, so `packets_delivered` there read `packets_read_game` (0 on a console whose game never reads) and the first run came back `observed:true, delivered:false`; fixed, and now guarded at session start by `md_link_stats_canary` (every field carries its declaration index, Rust checks each by name, and the size agrees) - a size check alone cannot see a permutation. Two more real defects were fixed on the way: the ledger is no longer zeroed by a wireless power-on (only by a bus reset - the read counters beside it are not zeroed either, and `rx 3614 / delivered 1360` in one log was a contradiction, not a finding), and **the button no longer powers the consoles' radios on and off under a running game**. That poke is what it used to do to get the consoles onto the bus, and it wedges a live game: reproduced 2/2, after one Link test a 2-console MPH walk never raised a radio in three attempts over three minutes (`mask 0x0`, no new begins), while the same walk with no test before it reached radios-up in 17 s and a joined guest in 24 s; injection is now a *bus* attach (`md_link_test_attach`) that touches no hardware and no mask bit, and after the fix one run goes test -> walk -> join -> START GAME -> INGAME with `deauth 0/0` at the moment gameplay starts. **Both named states pass, every direction, payload intact**: 2-console MPH at rest with radios off (both directions `sent/delivered/observed/payloadOk/tsOk`, `seenBy drain`, `logs/v9ab`, `logs/v9ad`) and 4-console diag (all twelve directions, `logs/v9w`); the games' own reads are untouched, with the ledger and the reads agreeing frame for frame in a live match (p1 `rx 1081(+274) [game 1080(+274), drain 1] · delivered 1081(+274)`). The verdict is also readable now: a passing result used to be overwritten by the once-a-second status poll within a second (only errors were sticky), so results are held 8 s and errors 6 s through one hold variable. Two smaller things recorded rather than changed: a double-click runs two *sequential* tests (Tauri serializes these commands - one run 104 ms, two "concurrent" invokes 268 ms - both passed, so no concurrency guard was kept), and the standing first-association instability is still there: the match above reached INGAME and the host's own game tore it down 15 s later (`deauth-sent 2`, both radios off - the recorded "rejoin holds, first association dies" question, `logs/v9ac_2p_match.log`). Validation: smoke **134 checks, 0 failures** (was 111; +23 for the wire-FCS rule, the probe's observation/refusal/disarm cases and the ledger's epoch), `lobby_page.py --selftest` 18/0, `py_compile` and `bash -n` clean, sav pins unchanged except MPH at `13f23439` (the sessions played it; others pinned), app quit through its own channel, raw logs gzipped. |

Three independent signs they were local, not routed: no link-trace frame precedes them (no sender), the TSF in their bodies differs per console, and every one of them was delivered in the "outside multiplayer" state. **What the host's game does hear from each guest**, from the delivery trace: the guests' association frames (1 each), their deauths (1 each), their data frames (1 each) - and every answer they wrote: `collected a reply from p2 for aid 1` **17,996 of 18,006** and `...from p3 for aid 2` **414 of 420** (99.9% and 98.6%), with `clientfail 12` in 18,007 rounds, `stale 0`, `blank 0`, `notlooking 0`. **The tool was wrong and is corrected.** `tools/scripts/beacon-party.py` counted *every* `fc=0080` delivery as a party beacon, so the 60-byte AP beacons landed in the old table's "0 names" rows; it now separates them by length (room beacon >= 140 bytes, always behind a bus frame and the only frame with a player table; own AP beacon < 140 bytes, SSID `melonAP`, no bus frame), reports the delivery's own state, and takes `--kind room` (default), `ap` or `all`. | 2026-09-30 | 0.1.9bh | **The multiplay bus now belongs to the consoles instead of to the process: each console owns its inbox, the host it is following, its answers and its own reply recorder, and one function decides what one console is told about another. The reply path - never checked anywhere before - now is, and the check caught a bug in this pass's own rewrite before it could be trusted (the same test reads 94 of 97 against the old bus and 97 of 97 against the new one). With it the two-console match is re-demonstrated live and at three consoles the host's own beacon carries three party entries for the first time on record, while the host's game keeps writing its round loop for the whole session (`cmds tx 18007`, `clientfail 12`, zero watchdog stops)** | Asked - on the audit's finding that every console in the process shared one packet FIFO, one reply FIFO and one `LastHostID`, so a second client's peer and reply state had no owner - to restructure the MP link so each console owns its client, peer and reply state and its own routing, with a single obvious place deciding what one console is told about another; to keep the interface the nine `PlatformBridge` call sites use; to leave everything outside the link alone; then to prove through real runs that the working two-console match still works and that at three consoles the host's own beacon party list carries three entries with the host's round loop still writing - or to name the value or path that caps it. **The structure the diff needed, and what it replaced.** The old bus was one process-wide record: one `MPPacketQueue` every console read at its own offset, one `MPReplyQueue` **one** host read, one `MPStatus`, one `LastHostID`, and one `MPHostinst` set by whichever console last *sent* a command - so an answer was filed by the arrival order of a shared queue and posted to whichever console spoke last, and "the console I am following" was one value for the whole process. What replaces it is per-console state plus one router, and that is now the file's own shape: `LocalMP::Console` (inbox + read cursor + `frameSem` + the host this console follows + its own `answers` queue + `answerSem` + its own `MPReplyDiag`) exists once per console, `LocalMP::Route` is the single place that decides who is told what (a frame goes to every attached console except its sender, as the air carries it; an answer goes to the console whose command its sender is following, because that is the only console it can belong to - and an answer from a console following nobody is dropped and named rather than credited to a host that merely spoke last), and `LocalMP::TrimAnswers` keeps the newest `MD_REPLY_KEEP` answers *per sender* instead of a `keep x peers` count handed to the queue as a whole. `Console::hostInst` is set where the old `LastHostID` was - when a console *reads* a command - so the peer state follows the game's own use of the frame. The frontend's interface is untouched: `Begin`/`End`/`Reset`, the seven send/receive calls, `GetReplyDiag` and the timeout/budget setters all compile against the same nine call sites in `PlatformBridge.cpp:579-699`. **The reply path had no test at all, and writing one found a real bug in this pass's own code.** The smoke test's link section covered broadcast delivery, isolation and detach, but nothing drove a command and an answer through the bus. It now does, against the bus class directly: a command from the host reaches both clients' own inboxes and not the host's; two clients answering one round arrive in one window under AIDs 1 and 2 in their own slots of the caller's buffer, with both credited in its recorder; an answer from a console that has never read a command is not filed anywhere; a client whose host leaves is told `-1` instead of waiting; a new command keeps the newest answer per client and counts the one it dropped; and two consoles hosting a client **each** have each answer collected by the console it is following and not the other. That last pair is what the old bus cannot express, and the A/B on the same test says so exactly: old `LocalMP.{h,cpp}` from `HEAD` **94 of 97, failing precisely the three ownership cases** (a follower-less answer credited to a host, and the two-groups case both ways); this pass's bus **97 of 97**. The check also caught the defect this pass had just written into `TrimAnswers`: the walk advanced by the entry's *header* instead of to the next entry, so a command with unread answers walked 1024 zero entries out of the queue, dropped them all and left the read cursor 24 KB past the data - **62 `REPLY FIFO OVERFLOW` lines in the first live two-console run, whose results were discarded for it**, and `reanchor-dropped 1023` in the test. Fixed, and the check kept. **The two-console match still works, live.** `logs/v9d_2p_app.log.gz` / `logs/v9d_2p_match.log`: the host hosts, the guest joins on its first try, the ladder is walked, the guest picks, the host's roster offers `START GAME` (`pill 414`) and the tap starts a **real match** - the host's own bottom screen reads `INGAME` with the match HUD's ink (951 / 2730 pixels, top colour animating 7400 to 22207) at t+5s and every 5 s to t+30s, `mask 0x3` and both consoles' own roles 1/2 for all of it, `deauth-sent 0` - and the host's round loop wrote about 59 commands per second with `collected == cmds tx`, `empty 0`, `stale 0`, `reanchor-dropped 0`, `blank 0`, `notlooking 0`, `clientfail 0`. The guest's own game then dropped it at t+35s (the recorded MPH behaviour after a match), and the app's watchdog **never fired once in the whole session** (`this console's own game has stopped sending commands` appears 0 times; the `host loop stopped` shorthand used below is that line). **The audit's ct2 finding is accounted for, and it is the failing lobby, not the link.** ct2's three stops came from a two-console lobby whose guest's pick never landed; this pass had exactly one such lobby - the first run (`logs/v9c_2p_app.log.gz`), 2 consoles, no match, 4 guest drop-and-rejoin cycles - and it produces the same watchdog shape, **5 stops**, while also showing the trim defect this pass wrote (62 `REPLY FIFO OVERFLOW`), which is why its results were discarded. In the two sessions that *did* reach a match the line never appears: 0 in `v9d` and 0 in `v9e`. **Three consoles: the host's own beacon lists three players, and the round loop keeps writing.** `logs/v9e_3p_app.log.gz` / `logs/v9e_3p_match.log` (150 s watch): both guests join on their first try, the host walks its ladder, both guests pick, `START GAME` starts a match, and the session ends with `mask 0x7`, p1 `role=1`, p2 and p3 `role=2` and one deauth in the whole run (a guest that left once and was walked back in). The host's own beacon - read back out of the console's own delivery trace with the new `tools/scripts/beacon-party.py`, which reassembles each 160-byte `fc=0080` frame from the `body[...]` rows and counts the room table's 16-byte name slots - **carries three entries**, for 53 frames in the window around the second join, 26 delivered to p2 and 27 to p3, every one of them sent by `0009BF112233` (the host's own MAC). This is the number the previous pass measured as **zero**: across five recorded three-console runs the host's beacon carried two names 190/1272/324/322/327 times and three names **never**. It then falls back to two names while the second guest is still attached, still `role=2` and still being credited (414 of the host's 18,007 rounds polled it, `collected a reply from p3 for aid 2`), which is a change in the host's *game* and not in the bus: the host's own view shows no link defect at all - `cmds tx 18007`, `collected 18410`, `clientfail 12` (0.07%), `empty 12`, `stale 0`, `blank 0`, `notlooking 0`, `reanchor-dropped 16`, `acks 18007`, and **zero watchdog stops**. The one host-side asymmetry measured next to it, for the next pass: the host's console delivers almost none of its clients' own beacons to its game - 35 `fc=0080` deliveries all run against the roughly 12,000 the two clients sent, while the *other guest* received 2,011 of them - so a host game that keeps a room entry alive by hearing a client has little to hear. That is recorded as a measurement and a candidate, not as the named cap: the result asked for (three entries, loop writing) is what the run shows, and both the party-list counts and the host's own delivery view are kept as `logs/v9e_3p_beacon-finding.txt`. **Validation:** smoke **97 checks, 0 failures** (`build-tauri/md_smoke_test tools/mkdiagrom/diag.nds`, 79 before the reply-path checks were added), the A/B above on the same test, `tools/scripts/beacon-party.py` added for the party-list reading, `lobby_page.py --selftest` **17 labelled checks, 0 wrong**, `py_compile` and `bash -n` clean, app quit through its own channel every time, no strays, raw app logs gzipped (`v9c` 41 MB to 4.2 MB, `v9d` 35 MB to 2.9 MB, `v9e` 116 MB to 12.6 MB), and the five `Test Roms/*.sav`: four at their pins and MPH **written by the completed matches** (the pin `ffa024d5...` was taken before the two-console run, the runs left `77a5b249...` then `91fa2064...`, and the pin was restored into place from `game-captures/sav-backups/...before-app-20260930-082523`). Nothing committed. Changed: `src/net/LocalMP.h`, `src/net/LocalMP.cpp` (per-console state and `Route`; `TrimAnswers` per sender; the struct and file comments that record the shape), `tools/coretest/smoke_test.cpp` (the reply-path checks), `tools/coretest/CMakeLists.txt` (the link header on the test's include path), `tools/scripts/beacon-party.py` (new), `history.md`, `to-do.md`, `compatibility.md`. |

Fixed in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` (`const IRQ_WIFI_IE7: u16 = 1 << (24 - 16)`) and re-read out of the archived runs' own raw `IE7` column: wifi enabled in **143/143 and 32/32** on-air samples in `arm7_a`, **328/333 and 226/226** in `arm7_b`; the live run's digest now prints `wifi ie yes` (73 samples, and the one `no` is an early `IE7 0004`). **The decision.** `Platform::MP_End` has exactly one caller - `Wifi::UpdatePowerOn`, reached only when the game clears wifi `POWCNT` - so nothing in the tree can end a session on its own, and the logs show the games doing it: in `arm7_b` the host's own game, immediately after a **fully successful** round (`collected=0002 of mask 0002`, the guest's reply aged 15751 of the 33430-tick budget, handoff OK), armed `LOC3` with a deauth to broadcast (`reason 0003`, 30 bytes) and then wrote TRX-power-off, RF idle channel registers and `POWCNT` clear -> `WIFI: OFF` at frame 7987; the guest's radio went at 7990 because our path **handed it the deauth** (`[mp] deauth received: ... reason 0003`, then `IsMPClient -> false`, then its own `WIFI: OFF`). In `arm7_a` the direction is the other way (guest deauths first at frame 4507 after its 1771st poll round, host follows at 4531, 11 frames after receiving the frame), so the direction is not a rule and the mechanism is. The 12,725-frame survivor never deauthed at all: its last wireless event in 963,135 lines is the guest turning its radio **on** at frame 3976. So the path is not the actor: it delivered the frame that ended the session, in both directions, and the survivor kept delivering for another 12,725 frames. **Ruled out by construction.** The reply window's carried-over unread answer, which v0.1.9h flagged as a stale-state hazard: `1 unread` at **22,853 of 22,853** rounds in the survivor and **4,792 of 4,799** in `arm7_b`, i.e. identical in the healthy and the failing session - it is the protocol's steady state, not the cause, and the next pass should not spend a run on it. **What remains, and where the fix is.** Both games sit in the same game-level keepalive while the link layer heartbeats (the host's command body changes only in a 4-byte field, the guest answers `00 80 5A 5A B8 B6` byte-identically for thousands of rounds) - the same wall as the park, now approached from the side that has a reproducer. | 2026-09-29 | 0.1.9as | **The park is a blocking dequeue in the game's own netcode: the expression it spins on is `*(u32*)0x2173300 == [0x21732AC]`, frozen *equal* on both consoles, and writing the word moves the console out of the wait in one step - what is missing is a message the dead radio will never post** | Asked to read the code at the pinned PC and the variables it reads, from each console's own memory, and then satisfy the condition or name it with evidence. **The PC is a `delay(100)` helper and the wait around it is now identified end to end.** `pc9 0200FD10` on 38-39 of 40 samples, disassembled live out of RAM (the ROM file's ARM9 image matches 0/256 words there, so the listing has to come from the console): `0200FD10 subs r0,r0,#4 / bcs` = the SDK's delay, `0200FCA0`/`0200FCB4` = IRQ disable/restore, `0200FD1C` a never-returning panic loop. A whole-4 MB `bl`-target scan (1.1 s, 1024 `mem` reads) finds 5 callers; three are retry loops inside one netcode module, and their exit conditions separate cleanly against live values read in the same window: (A) `0x214d79c(7,1)` = bit 7 of `[0x027FFC00+0x388+4]` = `0000FFF0`, **set**; (B) `0x214d6e8` returns -1 only while `IPCFIFOCNT` bit 14 is set, and `0x04000184` reads `8501`/`8601`, **clear**; (C) `*0x2173300 != [0x21732AC]`, and those two words are **equal** - 3F/3F on p1 and 32/32 on p2, the same pair in three separate runs (3F/3F and 32/32 in park5 and park6, 31/31 and 1F/1F earlier in the same session, i.e. state rather than noise). So the CPU can only be in (C). **The primitive is a queue dequeue, and its reader says who writes the value.** `0x2021488(arg)` with `arg & 1` is the blocking form: it calls `0x2021834()` (which is `DC_InvalidateRange(0x2173300,4)` - `0x200EE3C` is `mcr 15,0,r0,cr7,cr6,{1}` - followed by `*(u32*)0x2173300`), compares against `[0x21732AC]`, and `delay(100)`s between polls; when they differ it dequeues ring slot `[[0x21732CC] + idx*4]`, walks the entry's list and increments `[0x21732AC]` (`0x2021590`). Only four references to the pointer global `0x2174DE0` exist in 4 MB - the module's own init (which stores the buffer address `0x2173300` into it once) and three reads - and **none of them stores through it**, so the word the ARM9 polls is never written by ARM9 code - and invalidating the cache before reading it says the same thing a second way. **Satisfying the condition, directly.** Added the write half of `mem` to the control channel (`appctl.py poke <hex addr> <hex words>`, on the bridge's existing `md_write_ram` - the Rust app rebuilt, 2m03s), then on a parked guest: `pc9` `0200FD10` 37/40 -> `poke 2173300 00000000` (read back `00000000`) -> `pc9` `0200FD10` **0/40**, `02021544` 40/40 - the linked-list walk inside exactly the branch that test guards (`0x2021530`-`0x202154C`) - and `[0x21732AC]` `32` -> `33`, the dequeue's own increment. Twenty seconds later `*0x2173300` was **still** `00000000` while `[0x21732AC]` stayed `33`: nothing in the emulated machine writes the producer's word back. That is the whole finding - the console is held by the poll, the poll is held by a message that never arrives, and the queue it would dequeue from holds no live entry (the 9 ring pointers read `0x2173D00, 0x2, 0x5, 0x21E1A30, 0x953, 0x21DF880, ...`, and the one the poke consumed walked a list that does not terminate). Artifact: `logs/park7_evidence.txt` (annotated listings of the primitive, `f_2021834`, the three loops, both register tests, and both consoles' live values). **Why the message is missing, from the same window:** the guest's ARM7 sits in the BIOS with its wireless interrupt off (`arm7 IME 0001 IE7 0104 IF7 0000`, printed "wifi ie no, if no") and both games have stopped reading the wireless block - the healthy park's `handed seq` is frozen at `D0E0`/`DCE0` across every 15-frame report while `link mask` stays `0x3` for 12,725 frames - so the black screen is not a torn-down link and not a busy one: it is two games that have each stopped being fed. **The dead variant's screen, decoded rather than guessed** (there is no OCR on this box): the guest's bottom screen is a system message box with the glyphs "An error has occurred." and an OK button, read letter by letter from the framebuffer by column-ink segmentation (19 cells, 11-row capital `A` against 8-row lowercase, `e`-bar and `d`-stem cross-checked) - and that string is **nowhere in the 4 MB of ARM9 main RAM**, so MKDS's own text bank did not draw it. Also kept: CUT OFF is not the end - in park6 both consoles were still `ACTIVE` with 1436 commands collected at the `[after-cut-off]` read and the link only went `0x3 -> 0x0` ~130 frames later, so the pre-race wait outlives the press and the deauth follows the games, not the tap. Route B spends the accuracy instead: a built-in Download Play client - a small ARM9/ARM7 boot image, or the client half of the download protocol implemented - so a joiner accepts a child image and jumps into it with **no firmware at all**, which the tree is already shaped for because `SetupDirectBoot()` in `src/NDS.cpp` is exactly "start a program that is not a cartridge". **Route B is the one to take**, and not only on accuracy grounds: it asks nothing of the user and keeps the BIOS/firmware legal question off the players. **It is also the hardware rule's best answer, which was not obvious until it was written down:** the three consoles that joined a Download Play session are not running the full cartridge at all, they are running a small image the host sent over the air, so the feature that widens compatibility is also cheaper per console than the multi-card path - the widest game set and the cheapest four-player configuration are the same work. Milestone 4 is gated honestly: the instruments (the memory read is also how an arrived image gets confirmed), Milestone 2's per-instance routing (Download Play is one parent and *N* children, so a machine-global `MPHostinst` cannot route two joiners), and its own bulk-transfer measurement, because a download is a large one-shot transfer rather than the small polled exchange `MD_REPLY_KEEP`'s backlog and the reply budget were tuned for. Four closing items, in order: a 2-console single-card join on Star Fox Command (host half proven), the download asserted from the joiner's own RAM, Super Mario 64 DS's VS mode, then the same at four players. **The hardware rule is now its own section** ("4 players, on the weakest machine that can hold them"): frames are the budget, work scaling with the console count has to justify itself, prefer *not doing* a console's work over doing it faster, the link must never depend on wall-clock slack (on a weak machine the clock stretches and behaviour that changes with the machine is the worst couch co-op failure), cheap accuracy-for-frames wins are in scope, and every claim under it is a measurement on the weakest machine available. `AGENTS.md` carries both the Download Play item and the hardware rule in its DO list, and `compatibility.md`'s Download Play section is retitled "planned, not supported yet" with the ⛔ rows for Star Fox Command and Super Mario 64 DS marked "blocked today (planned)" - and its "Adding a title" step now says every ⛔ row becomes worth re-running once Milestone 4 lands. **One precision found while writing it, which makes the top instrument cheaper than the roadmap implied:** a memory *read* is not new emulator work at all - `md_read_ram(inst, addr, out, len)` already exists in the bridge (`Bridge.cpp`, 4-byte alignment enforced) and the smoke test already proves it; only `md_write_ram` is declared on the Rust side (declared for the diag identity block). The sampler now runs *inside the page* (`SAMPLER_JS`, sent as one `eval` - the channel is not asked per sample): one `__mdctl.frames()` per iteration, 64-74 samples/s against a 59.8 fps session, and a pair is counted **only when both consoles advanced exactly one emulated frame**, so every number is the game's own one-frame delta and not the sampler's. Each phase returns a bitmask per console and screen of every pixel that moved (one bit per pixel, so the intersection and the difference are byte operations) plus per-pair moved-pixel counts; the two no-input phases are the baseline, and the question is which pixels moved under one console's input and in *neither* baseline. Four phases of 4 s: A no input, B the guest alone holds RIGHT, C no input, **D the host alone holds RIGHT** - the mirror is what makes a null readable ("the host's screen does not show the guest" and "the host's screen does not respond to anything in this state" look identical without it). **Where each input landed is joined on the frame, not on phase order:** each phase reports the frames its pairs covered and the app's own `[input] frame N player P` trace says when every line went out, so "the host sent nothing in the guest's phase" is checked - phase B owns `p1 1` and the host's trace is empty there, phase D owns `p2 2`. **The measurement, four runs (12:00, 12:07, 12:14 - the third after every fix, `logs/nsmb_join_fine_canon.log` - and 12:22, which also ran the coarse test).** Roles read off the digest, not assumed: `{1: 'guest', 2: 'host'}`. Identical shape each time, input-only pixels (top, bottom) - the guest's own screens under the guest's input: **(30512, 1927)**, **(29000, 1895)**, **(31679, 1874)**; the host's screens under the guest's input: **(1021, 1927)**, **(1739, 1910)**, **(1021, 1857)**; the host's own screens under the host's input: (32632, 1943), (31311, 1880), (35361, 1879); the guest's screens under the host's input: (526, 1943), (385, 1842), (582, 1874). A fourth run (12:22) reads (18581, 711) / **(0, 740)** / (31595, 1747) / (627, 1746): the guest walked less in it, and the host's *top view* did not move at all while the shared map still did - so the robust half of this result is the shared map and the mirror, and the host's own view reacting is three runs out of four, not four. The no-input baselines for the host's top screen were 611 and 612 px over a whole 4 s phase - i.e. ~3 px per frame, against 1021-1739 px that never appeared there - and for **both consoles' bottom screens, 0 px in every baseline**. The top-screen change sits in a band at y 155..178 (bbox (199,155)-(255,178) and (167,155)-(255,178)) and the mirror's on the guest's side is the same band (x 0..200), which is the game's own layout, not a sampler artifact. The 2x2 is the whole argument: each console's own view responds to its own input (18,500-31,700 px, and 31,600-35,400 px for the host), each responds to the *other's* input in a small, localized way, and neither cross-effect is an animation the baselines happened to miss because the mirror is the same shape (the guest's pictures move 385-627 px under the host's input in all four runs). **The bottom screen moved in all four runs (1927, 1910, 1857, 740 px) and the host's own top view in three of them (1021, 1739, 1021, 0)** - the fourth run's guest was walking less, so its cross-effect is smaller everywhere, which is what a causal effect should look like and is why the run's own report leads with the 2x2 rather than with a single number. **The shared map, stated precisely.** The two consoles' bottom screens differed by **0 px per pair** in both input phases (and by 0 px in the run's own capture, `game-means 191.8` both), while their top screens differed by 24,000-26,600 px per pair - so the bottom screen is *one* picture both consoles render, and its 1,857-1,927 px is one picture moving rather than two views agreeing. The attribution to the host's *game* therefore rests on two things together: the host console renders that picture from its own game state, and its own top view moved 1,021 px under the guest's input alone. The counters were healthy throughout (`link mask 0x3`, guest `answered 12104`, host `collected 12100 empty 0 clientfail 4`, `deauth 0/0`). **Three defects in this pass's own instrument, all found by being wrong rather than by inspection.** (1) **The verdict claimed attribution from noise.** `if sum(cross) > 0` printed `attributed ... 11 px` for a session ten minutes on whose guest's own screens had moved **6 px** under the guest's own RIGHT - a state that cannot answer the question at all. The control now gates the claim (the pressed console's own screens must move >= 100 px) and the cross-effect must reach 100 px, both far above that noise floor and far below the 29,000-31,700 px the working runs show; the refusal was then exercised on real data (a follow-up run on the same session: control **1 px**, `VERDICT: inconclusive ... nothing is claimed about the peer`). Without this gate the pass would have shipped a claim built on eleven pixels. (2) **The input attribution window was too tight.** A line is logged when the channel applies it, and the sampler's first pair is a frame or two later, so the host's own RIGHT press (frame 14907) fell outside the phase D window (14908..15145) and the run reported "input lines in that window: none" for the very press it was looking for - which reads as "the host sent nothing during its own input phase". Lines are now attributed to the nearest phase window within 8 frames (`INPUT_JOIN_SLACK`), and the gap between windows here is under 3 frames. (3) **A one-letter shadow crashed the analysis:** the attribution loop reused `d` for its frame distance, overwriting phase D's report, and the next use of it died with `'int' object is not subscriptable` (renamed `gap`). (4) **"Nearest window" mis-attributed a press across a phase boundary.** A line takes effect a frame or two before its phase's sampling starts and a frame or two after the previous phase's ends, so distance alone is the wrong rule: the host's own RIGHT for phase D logged at frame 15271 against C ending at 15270 and D starting at 15273, and the report read "phase C owns p2 1" - input inside a no-input baseline that the driver never sent. Lines are now attributed only when they fall *inside* a phase's covered frames, everything else is printed as "at a phase boundary ... (nearest window X)", and the per-console totals with their frame ranges are printed so the claim is checkable by eye (a re-run printed `the guest console's own input over the whole measurement: 2 line(s), frames 24678..24919` against `the host console's own input: 1 line(s), frames 25160..25160` - the host's only line is 481 frames after the guest's phase closed). **The coarse test it replaces was also wrong, and had been for a pass:** `couple_test` hard-coded player 2 as the guest and player 1 as the host, so it pressed the *host* while reporting "the guest alone holds RIGHT", then counted the *guest's* input lines to prove "the host sent nothing" - on this ROM the digest says the opposite (`p1 [guest]`, `p2 [host]`). It now reads the roles off the digest. **What is still open, and what this does not claim.** A value out of NSMB's own RAM for "the peer's world changed" - the app still has no memory-read command, so the full-frame instrument is what exists. And the measurement needs the game in the race: ten minutes later in the same session the guest's RIGHT no longer moved the guest's own screen (1 px), which the run now reports as `inconclusive` rather than as a null about the app; future runs re-drive the route rather than reusing an old session. **Validation and hygiene.** `python3 -m py_compile tools/nsmb_join.py`, smoke **66 checks, 0 failures** (the C++ core is untouched), and the runs themselves - four measurements across four app sessions, three of them attributed and one deliberately refused. No emulator, link, transport, clock, cropping or touch-coordinate line was touched, and the app was not rebuilt: the only changed source is `tools/nsmb_join.py` (the sampler, the four phases, the frame-joined input attribution, the control gate, the roles fix), plus `BUILD.md`, `to-do.md` and this row. At `MD_REPLY_TIMEOUT_MS=2`: **0** windows were budget-capped (the wait was always over before the budget could be consulted) and **394** closed empty. The counters move with them, which is what makes them the peer's replies rather than the host's arithmetic: at 2ms the host read `cmds tx 9815 acks 9815 (clientfail 394)`, `collected 9419 empty 394 reanchor-dropped 393` while the guest `answered 9812 (+ canned 1) = replies sent 9813` - the answers were sent, arrived late, and were thrown away by the next command; at 34ms the same counters read 43/43/42 over the part of the route both arms reached. Both arms also ended with `deauth out 1` on both consoles, so that is route timing rather than the ceiling (v0.1.9af's run of this route read `deauth 0/0`), and the arms are not matched in length - the 2ms arm ran ~3.7x more rounds in the same wall clock because it never blocks, and its loss rate climbs with the game's own retry storm (1/239 = 0.4% in the first window, 154/1800 = 8.6% in the last). **So: not costing anything, and here is what the 34ms protects.** It is the amount a *window* may spend before the budget is consulted, and it is deliberately above the budget (34ms against 33.4ms of two emulated frames) so that the budget - not the ceiling - is what decides a window; at that setting the ceiling never bound once in 2682 rounds. It exists because a client's answer goes out at the client's own frame boundary, up to one **emulated frame** of wall clock after the host asks (16.73ms at the DS's 59.826Hz), and the harness's 2ms is affordable only because the harness runs at 4.78x realtime where a frame is ~3.5ms; the app paces to real time. Set the ceiling below the frame's own slack, as `MD_REPLY_TIMEOUT_MS=2` does, and the host hangs up with 31ms of its own unused budget in hand - 394 times on one MKDS route. **Three defects on that path, all fixed at the cause.** (1) The budget knob was called `MD_REPLY_BUDGET_MS` and its value was read as a **frame count**, so a sweep at `17` bought 17 frames (284ms) and a sweep at `-1` bought the 2-frame default rather than the "no cap" the docs promised; it is now `MD_REPLY_BUDGET_FRAMES`, takes emulated frames, and a negative value means unbounded, which is what `MD_REPLY_BUDGET_NS=-1` already meant in the harness. (2) `tools/run_app.sh` forwarded `MD_REPLY_TIMEOUT_MS` but **not** the budget variable at all - the app reads its environment directly and an unforwarded variable never arrives - so the one knob the previous pass wanted to sweep could not be set through the app's own launcher; both are forwarded and documented in the script's header now. (3) The startup line printed `link reply timeout: {N}ms (2 emulated frames at 59.826fps)` for **every** value, so this pass's own first reading of its 2ms arm had 2ms described as two frames when it is an eighth of one; it now says `link reply timeout: 34ms = 2.03 emulated frames (default)` and names `MD_REPLY_TIMEOUT_MS` when that is where the value came from. **Boundaries, through the launcher, four ways:** default, `0`, `-1` and `abc` each started two consoles on the diag ROM and each ran ~600 frames, logging `budget: 2 emulated frame(s)` / `0` / `unbounded (MD_REPLY_BUDGET_FRAMES<0)` / `2` respectively - the junk value falling back to the default rather than to 0 or to unbounded (`logs/budget_*.log`). **The re-proof, on the rebuilt binary and the default settings.** Built `cargo build --release` (2m03s: the crate is the only thing that changed), then the app's own NSMB route via `tools/nsmb_join.py --restart --couple --shots-at 3780,4200,5000`. The coupled state survives: guest `cmds rx 14503 answered 14503 (blank 0, canned 0)` with `replies sent 14503 arms 14501`, host `cmds tx 14503 acks 14503` with `collected 14499 empty 4 stale 0 reanchor-dropped 3`, `deauth out 0 in 0` on **both** consoles and **0** `[link-event]` deauth lines in the run, **`link mask 0x3`**, both consoles still running at frame 17602 after 294s when it was quit, and one control-channel read showing both consoles coupling the guest's own input (`couple`: 2.5s of nothing gives 10 distinct pictures per console, then the guest alone holds RIGHT for 2.5s with **0** host input lines in the window and both consoles' screens ending on the same checksum, `p1:e7f2acc5 p2:e7f2acc5` - NSMB's shared-screen shape). The playable state is on screen and in this pass's own captures: `run9-f3780.png` / `run9-f5000.png` (canvas mean **164.6** and **164.5**) against `nsmb-f2012.png` / `nsmb-f2600.png` at **120.6** / **123.1** for the dialog screens, kept in `game-captures/nsmb-app/`. **Saves.** The supervisor's pin announced the session (`sav pinned before the session: ... md5 bdbb2dfe...`), reported `sav ... unchanged (md5 bdbb2dfe...)` and `app exited with status 0` at the end, and all five `Test Roms/*.sav` are byte-identical to the pinned originals (`identical` for MKDS `5e180fda`, LEGO `5af705e8`, MPH `ffa024d5`, NSMB `bdbb2dfe`, SM64DS `ad192e5d`). **What is still open, with the number that bounds it.** A four-round startup transient: at the same frames the app loses 4 of 2221 rounds and the harness 0 of 2325; it is 4 at the default ceiling and 1 at 2ms, so no reply knob causes it, it does not grow after frame ~4081 over 14,503 commands, and the guest answers every command it receives - so the answers exist and are dropped host-side, 3 of them arriving after the host had moved on (`reanchor-dropped 3`). The rounds in question are the first few after the guest's radio comes up (frame ~2735; the counts move between the 3580 and 4081 digests), which is the next measurement this file records. **Validation and hygiene.** Smoke **66 checks, 0 failures** (the C++ core is untouched; only the app crate and one shell launcher changed), `python3 -m py_compile` on the three drivers, `bash -n tools/run_app.sh`, `sh -n tools/scripts/nsmb-route.sh`, and the boundary launches above. Changed: `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` (the budget variable's name, unit and negative case; the timeout's log line), `tools/run_app.sh` (forward the budget variable; document both), `BUILD.md` (the two reply-tuning bullets, now saying which knob binds and what the ceiling protects), `to-do.md` (the sweep item closed with the verdict; the transient added as the next measurement), this row. No link, transport, clock, cropping or touch-coordinate line was touched, and no harness source changed. Evidence kept: `logs/mkds_timeout_arms_keylines.log` (both arms: every window closure, the budget-capped windows, both roles' digests), `logs/nsmb_app_run9_keylines.log` (the re-proof's digests, input trace and link events - the full 96M log was deleted), `logs/nsmb_harness_frame5200.log`, `logs/nsmb_run9.driver.txt`, `logs/mkds_app_join3.log` / `logs/mkds_app_join4.log` (the two arms' route timelines), `logs/budget_{DEFAULT,0,-1,abc}.log`, and the captures named above.
